An informed monotone neural network for multi-stage chronic kidney disease prediction with integrated GFR estimation
Authors: Bikram Pratim Bhuyan, Akeel Sh. Mahmoud, Olfa Lamouchi, Galina Ivanova, Amar Ramdane-Cherif
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
Chronic kidney disease (CKD) is a progressive condition affecting over 850 million people worldwide, where timely detection and accurate staging are critical to reducing morbidity, mortality, and healthcare burden. Current machine learning approaches often treat CKD prediction as a binary task, neglecting the ordered nature of disease stages, and rarely incorporate physiological constraints or calibrated probability estimates essential for clinical decision support. We propose a multi-stage CKD prediction framework that integrates ordinal classification, probability calibration, and a serum creatinine-monotonicity constraint within a knowledge distillation paradigm. A calibrated CatBoost model serves as a teacher, transferring temperature-scaled class probabilities to an ordinal neural network student augmented with an auxiliary eGFR regression task. Evaluated on a cohort of 750 patients from Al-Ramadi Teaching Hospital, our approach achieved superior macro-F1 and reduced expected calibration error. This work demonstrates that combining ordinal learning, calibration, and physiological constraints yields models that are not only accurate but also aligned with clinical reasoning, offering a pathway toward safer and more trustworthy AI tools for CKD management. Limitations like circular reasoning and target leakage are also discussed.

Introduction

Chronic kidney disease (CKD) is a progressive, often silent condition affecting more than 850 million people worldwide, making it one of the leading contributors to global morbidity and mortality 1. Driven by ageing populations and the rising prevalence of diabetes and hypertension, CKD incidence continues to increase, exerting a substantial burden on patients, healthcare systems, and economies. In early stages, CKD is largely asymptomatic; by the time clinical symptoms emerge, kidney damage is typically irreversible. Early detection and precise staging are therefore critical to slow disease progression, reduce complications such as cardiovascular events, and optimise treatment decisions.

Clinical practice guidelines, such as those from the Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes (KDIGO) consortium 2, recommend staging CKD using both the estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) and albuminuria. The eGFR, calculated from serum creatinine (Scr) via equations such as the CKD Epidemiology Collaboration (CKD-EPI) or the Modification of Diet in Renal Disease (MDRD) formula 3,4, remains a cornerstone for diagnosis, medication dosing, and referral timing. The KDIGO classification includes six severity levels (stages 1-5, with stage 3 split into 3a and 3b) that are inherently ordered, and misclassifying between non-adjacent stages can have significant clinical consequences.

Machine learning (ML) offers the potential to improve CKD detection and staging from routine clinical data, yet three persistent challenges limit current approaches: Stage-aware modelling is underused Most studies reduce CKD prediction to a binary “disease vs. no disease” task, discarding the ordinal nature of KDIGO staging and increasing the likelihood of large-step misclassifications that matter in practice.Physiological plausibility is rarely enforced Even high-performing models may produce biologically implausible predictions; for example, assigning a milder CKD stage despite higher serum creatinine; undermining clinician trust.Probability calibration is neglected Overconfident or poorly calibrated outputs can mislead clinical decision-making, especially when model predictions are integrated into triage or treatment algorithms.Recent advances in ordinal classification 5,6 exploit the inherent order of labels to improve discrimination between adjacent categories. Probability calibration techniques, such as temperature scaling 7,8, can align predicted probabilities with observed frequencies, reducing the risk of harmful overconfidence. Knowledge distillation 9,10 enables the transfer of predictive capability from a complex, well-calibrated teacher model to a simpler, faster student model, suitable for clinical deployment.

Stage-aware modelling is underused Most studies reduce CKD prediction to a binary “disease vs. no disease” task, discarding the ordinal nature of KDIGO staging and increasing the likelihood of large-step misclassifications that matter in practice.

Physiological plausibility is rarely enforced Even high-performing models may produce biologically implausible predictions; for example, assigning a milder CKD stage despite higher serum creatinine; undermining clinician trust.

Probability calibration is neglected Overconfident or poorly calibrated outputs can mislead clinical decision-making, especially when model predictions are integrated into triage or treatment algorithms.

Moreover tabular medical data presents a unique challenge: tree-based ensembles (e.g., CatBoost, XGBoost, LightGBM) typically outperform neural networks on small-to-medium datasets due to sample efficiency and native handling of mixed-type features 11; however, despite their superior discrimination on tabular benchmarks, these ensemble models lack three properties essential for clinical deployment: i) Differentiability: Tree-based ensembles are piecewise-constant functions that do not support gradient-based constraints. We cannot enforce physiological monotonicity (e.g., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \text {stage}/\partial \text {Scr} \ge 0$$\end{document}) via gradient penalties in CatBoost, whereas neural networks naturally support such constraints through automatic differentiation. ii) Compact deployment: Ensemble models require 100–700 trees, each storing split thresholds, feature indices, and leaf values, resulting in models >100 MB. This is unsuitable for edge devices (mobile health applications, resource-constrained clinics) or real-time inference in clinical workflows where sub-millisecond latency is required. iii) Ordinal structure: Tree-based models treat output classes independently, with no mechanism to encode that CKD stages 1–6 are ordered. This can lead to large-step misclassifications (predicting Stage 1 when truth is Stage 5) that are clinically catastrophic.

Knowledge distillation 9,12 addresses these limitations by transferring the ensemble’s learned patterns into a compact, differentiable neural network. The teacher (calibrated CatBoost) provides rich supervision via soft probability distributions. These softened probabilities expose knowledge about relationships between neighboring CKD stages (e.g., Stage 3a vs 3b are more similar than Stage 1 vs 6); this information would be completely lost if we transferred only hard argmax labels 13. The student (ordinal neural network) can combine this transferred knowledge with architectural inductive biases: (i) an ordinal head that enforces stage ordering, reducing large-step errors; (ii) an auxiliary eGFR regression task that anchors predictions in continuous kidney function; and (iii) a serum creatinine-monotonicity penalty that ensures physiological consistency. This combination of ensemble discrimination, neural network flexibility, and domain-knowledge constraints forms the foundation of our approach. Used separately, calibration can’t fix violations of physical laws; an ordinal structure without calibration yields overconfident predictions. Our integrated framework ensures predictions are simultaneously accurate, well-ordered, calibrated, and physiologically consistent.

To our knowledge, no prior CKD staging framework integrates these three components – (i) ordinal learning, (ii) calibration, and (iii) domain-specific physiological constraints – into a single, coherent approach. In this study, we address these gaps by developing an informed monotonic neural network for multi-stage CKD prediction. Our framework combines:Ordinal regression (CORN) to respect the KDIGO stage ordering,Probability-space knowledge distillation from a calibrated CatBoost teacher to improve generalisation on limited data,An auxiliary eGFR regression task to anchor predictions in continuous kidney function,A serum creatinine-monotonicity penalty to enforce physiological consistency.We evaluate our approach on a single-centre registry study of 750 patients from Al-Ramadi Teaching Hospital, Iraq, and compare it against strong baseline models across both discrimination and calibration metrics. Our results demonstrate that integrating clinical priors with modern ML techniques yields stage predictions that are not only accurate but also calibrated and physiologically plausible; these properties are essential for safe and trustworthy AI-assisted CKD management.

Related works

The prediction and staging of chronic kidney disease (CKD) have been widely studied using both traditional machine learning (ML) and deep learning (DL) approaches. The UCI CKD dataset14, consisting of 400 patient records and 24 clinical attributes, has been the most frequently used benchmark for binary CKD detection. Classical ML models such as logistic regression (LR), decision trees (DT), k-nearest neighbours (KNN), support vector machines (SVM), random forests (RF), and gradient boosting (e.g., XGBoost) have consistently achieved high accuracies on this dataset when paired with proper preprocessing and feature selection. Islam et al.15 compared twelve ML classifiers for early CKD detection, finding XGBoost to achieve 98.33% accuracy, which improved to 99.16% after applying principal component analysis (PCA) for dimensionality reduction.

Sawhney et al.16 developed a multilayer perceptron (MLP) with feature extraction and selection for CKD detection using the UCI dataset. Their model achieved perfect accuracy on the test set, surpassing LR (96%) and SVM (82%), though the small dataset size raises concerns about overfitting. Alsekait et al.17 proposed an ensemble deep learning framework combining recurrent neural network (RNN), long short-term memory (LSTM), and gated recurrent unit (GRU) models with an SVM meta-learner. After feature selection via chi-square, recursive feature elimination (RFE), and mutual information, the method achieved 99.69% accuracy, demonstrating the potential of hybrid architectures.

While many works focus on binary detection18,19, several studies have addressed multi-class staging and progression. Rady and Anwar20 used probabilistic neural networks (PNN), MLP, SVM, and radial basis function (RBF) networks on a cleaned subset of the UCI dataset (361 records) for KDIGO-based CKD staging. The PNN achieved 96.7% accuracy, outperforming other models. Xiao et al.21 investigated CKD severity prediction in a hospital cohort of 551 patients using nine ML algorithms, including LR, elastic net, RF, and XGBoost. LR achieved the highest area under the ROC curve (AUC) of 0.873; elastic net achieved the highest sensitivity (0.85), and XGBoost obtained the highest specificity (0.83). These results show that well-calibrated linear models can be competitive in clinical settings. Fatima et al.22 developed a novel imputation technique for missing values, and the Deep Neural Network (DNN) improves the CKD model’s predictive performance.

A recent systematic review by Iliyas et al.23 synthesised 86 studies from 2019-2024, concluding that while DL models such as convolutional neural networks (CNNs) and MLPs can capture complex feature interactions, tuned tree-based ensembles like LightGBM, CatBoost, and XGBoost often outperform DL models on small-to-medium tabular datasets due to sample efficiency and interpretability. The review emphasised the importance of patient-level data splitting to avoid leakage, multiclass KDIGO staging (including the 3a/3b subdivision), and explainability techniques such as SHAP and LIME to improve clinical trust.

Ordinal classification methods have been proposed for tasks where class labels are ordered, as in CKD staging. Shi et al.5 introduced the CORN framework, which enforces rank consistency through conditional probabilities, achieving superior results to earlier methods such as CORAL6 across benchmark datasets. Integrating such approaches in CKD staging helps leverage the natural order of disease stages and reduce misclassifications between adjacent stages.

Model calibration is another critical aspect in medical AI. Guo et al.7 demonstrated that modern neural networks tend to be miscalibrated, and that post-hoc temperature scaling can significantly reduce expected calibration error (ECE)24 without affecting accuracy. Proper calibration is particularly important in CKD models to ensure that predicted probabilities reflect true disease likelihoods, supporting safe clinical decision-making.

Knowledge distillation (KD) has been explored to transfer knowledge from complex, high-performing models to simpler or smaller models. Frosst and Hinton25 distilled a deep neural network into a soft decision tree for improved interpretability. Borisov et al.10 reviewed methods for distilling gradient boosting decision trees (GBDTs) into neural networks for tabular data, showing that probability-based KD can improve student performance on small datasets while retaining efficiency.

Methods

Dataset acquisition and characteristics

A single-centre registry was established at Al-Ramadi Teaching Hospital (Iraq) from January to October 2024 and yielded n = 750 adult encounters. Each record contains fifteen attributes: thirteen candidate predictors and two response/predicted fields (estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR), and the physician-assigned CKD stage; full definitions in Table 1). Comprehensive description of the fifteen attributes in the Al-Ramadi CKD cohort. Ranges reflect typical physiological limits. “calc.” indicates values derived during pre-processing.

Missingness was low overall: Glucose 4.0%, Uric acid 2.7%, Serum creatinine 1.3%; all remaining variables were complete. Direct identifiers were stripped at source; the hospital’s ethics committee classified the project as non-human-subjects research, waiving informed consent. The de-identified dataset will be made available upon reasonable request under a CC-BY 4.0 licence (see “Data availability”). A histogram of the distribution of CKD stages is shown in Fig. 1.

It is important to note that complete KDIGO 2012 staging requires both eGFR categories (G1-G5) and albuminuria categories (A1-A3). Our dataset does not include albuminuria measurements due to limited routine testing at Al-Ramadi Teaching Hospital. Consequently, this study performs eGFR-grade classification along the eGFR axis only, not full KDIGO staging.

Despite being incomplete from a full KDIGO perspective, eGFR-grade classification remains clinically meaningful and practically relevant for four key reasons: i) Real-World Data Availability: Albuminuria testing (urine ACR) is not routinely performed in many healthcare settings. In contrast, serum creatinine is part of standard metabolic panels in >95% of healthcare systems; it is also inexpensive ($5-10 per test) and widely available. ii) eGFR Dominates Immediate Clinical Decisions: While complete KDIGO staging includes both markers, eGFR is the primary determinant for most immediate clinical actions including medication dosing (antibiotics, anticoagulants, chemotherapy etc.), nephrology referral, dialysis planning, kidney transplant evaluation etc. Albuminuria primarily refines long-term risk stratification for progression and cardiovascular events but does not typically change immediate management for stages G1-G4.Fig. 1Distribution of CKD stages in the Al-Ramadi cohort. A histogram of the six KDIGO-defined stages shows the class imbalance in the dataset, providing context for the macro-averaged performance metrics.

Distribution of CKD stages in the Al-Ramadi cohort. A histogram of the six KDIGO-defined stages shows the class imbalance in the dataset, providing context for the macro-averaged performance metrics.

Data preprocessing

Imputation strategy

Rows with more than three missing values did not occur; remaining gaps were imputed once by predictive mean matching (PMM, five donor draws)26. PMM preserves the empirical distribution of skewed laboratory variables more faithfully than mean or median substitution and has been shown to reduce downstream bias compared with deterministic single-value methods27.

Feature scaling and encoding

Continuous predictors were z-standardised using means and standard deviations. Binary predictors (Gender, Hypertension, Diabetes mellitus) were encoded as 0/1.Fig. 2Feature correlation matrix.

Feature correlation matrix.

Exploratory correlation analysis

Spearman correlation coefficients were calculated to identify collinearity (Fig. 2). The strongest positive association was observed between Urea and Serum creatinine (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho = 0.76$$\end{document}), while Urea and Haematocrit exhibited the strongest negative relationship (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho = -0.50$$\end{document}). These findings informed feature-importance interpretation but did not prompt feature removal because tree-based baselines are robust to moderate multicollinearity.

GFR estimation and stage labeling

Accurate estimation of glomerular filtration rate (GFR) is essential because chronic kidney disease (CKD) is often clinically silent until filtration falls well below normal values. Serum creatinine (Scr) (a low-molecular-weight waste product cleared almost exclusively by the kidneys) forms the basis of most estimating equations. Two formulae are in common use:

CKD-EPI formula

The revised Chronic Kidney Disease Epidemiology Collaboration equation3 expresses eGFR as1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{eGFR}_{\text {CKD-EPI}} = 142 \times \left[ \min \!\Bigl (\tfrac{\textrm{Scr}}{\kappa },1\Bigr )\right] ^{\alpha } \left[ \max \!\Bigl (\tfrac{\textrm{Scr}}{\kappa },1\Bigr )\right] ^{-1.200} \times 0.9938^{\text {Age}} \times (1.012)^{\text {female}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 0.7 & \text {female}\\ 0.9 & \text {male} \end{array}\right. }, \quad \alpha = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} -0.241 & \text {female}\\ -0.302 & \text {male} \end{array}\right. },$$\end{document}Scr in mg dL\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{-1}$$\end{document} and Age in years. The min-max construction applies a gentle slope (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document}) when Scr is below the sex-specific pivot \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa$$\end{document} and a steeper inverse slope (-1.200) above, thereby reducing proportional error across the physiological Scr range. The coefficient 1.012 accounts for lower baseline creatinine production in women; the 2009 race multiplier has been removed.

MDRD formula

The Modification of Diet in Renal Disease (MDRD) study group proposed an empirically calibrated alternative4:2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{eGFR}_{\text {MDRD}} = 175 \times \text {Scr}^{-1.154} \; \text {Age}^{-0.203} \; (0.742)^{\text {female}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where Scr and Age retain the same units. The female coefficient 0.742 corrects for lower muscle-derived Scr; the original African-American term (1.212) is omitted because the present cohort is ethnically homogeneous. Independent evaluations in Middle-Eastern cohorts suggest that MDRD yields smaller bias than CKD-EPI28 at eGFR < 60 mL min\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{-1}$$\end{document} 1.73 m\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{2}$$\end{document}. Additionally, CKD-EPI 2021 uses slightly different creatinine breakpoints optimized for newer assays; our creatinine values were measured using the Jaffé method standardized to IDMS, which aligns better with MDRD’s calibration. We therefore adopted Eq. 2 for all downstream analyses. Computed values were truncated to the physiological range 0-120 mL min\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{-1}$$\end{document} 1.73 m\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{2}$$\end{document}.

Equation 2 was applied row-wise, and resulting values were truncated to 0-120 mL min\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{-1}$$\end{document} 1.73 m\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{2}$$\end{document} to remove physiologically implausible outliers introduced by imputation. All computations used Scr values traceable to IDMS (isotope-dilution mass spectrometry) calibration. Following KDIGO 2012 guidelines2, eGFR was discretised into six ordinal CKD stages (Table 2). The transformed stage label replaces the original binary CKD flag and is the primary target for subsequent modelling; treating stage prediction as a six-class classification problem.Table 2KDIGO 2012 chronic-kidney-disease stage thresholds2 applied to derived eGFR values.StageeGFR range (mL min\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{-1}$$\end{document} 1.73 m\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{2}$$\end{document})Clinical interpretationStage label1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ge 90$$\end{document}Normal / hyperfiltration1260-89Mild reduction23A45-59Mild-moderate reduction33B30-44Moderate-severe reduction4415-29Severe reduction55\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<15$$\end{document}Kidney failure6

Machine learning framework

Baseline learners

To contextualize the design of the proposed informed neural network, we first established a comprehensive suite of baseline classifiers that collectively span the major algorithmic families for tabular data11: linear (logistic regression), distance-based (kNN), probabilistic (Gaussian naïve Bayes), kernel (RBF-SVM) and tree/boosting ensembles (Random Forest, Extra Trees, Gradient Boosting, XGBoost, LightGBM and CatBoost). Hyper-parameters were fixed a-priori as given in Table 3. All baselines are trained once on the full 525-row training set (70% training split with the random seed fixed to 42) and evaluated once on the 225-row hold-out test set; no internal cross-validation is performed. Class weights were not applied at this stage, permitting a fair comparison of raw discrimination capacity. They address the classification task only as continuous eGFR regression is unique to the proposed network and therefore omitted for the comparators. Performance was quantified with a set of scalar measures. For clarity we recall their definitions.Table 3Hyper-parameters used for the ten reference classifiers. Performance metrics

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_i \in \{1,\dots ,6\}$$\end{document} denote the true CKD stage label of encounter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{y}_i$$\end{document} the stage predicted by a classifier. The associated class-probability vector is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{p}_i=(p_{i1},\dots ,p_{i6})$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{ik}= \Pr (\text {stage}=k \,|\, \textbf{x}_i)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{k=1}^{6} p_{ik}=1$$\end{document}. From the predictions of all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N$$\end{document} test encounters we form the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$6{\times }6$$\end{document} confusion matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}=[C_{kj}]$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{kj}$$\end{document} counts cases of true class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k$$\end{document} predicted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j$$\end{document}. The following six scalar metrics are derived from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}$$\end{document} or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{p}_i$$\end{document}.

Accuracy (Acc).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Acc} = \frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^{N}\! \mathbb {1}\{y_i=\hat{y}_i\}.$$\end{document}The overall proportion of correctly classified stages is simple but sensitive to class imbalance.

Balanced accuracy (BalAcc).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{BalAcc} = \frac{1}{6}\sum _{k=1}^{6}\frac{C_{kk}}{\sum _{j=1}^{6} C_{kj}}.$$\end{document}Computes the per-class recall mean; gives each stage equal influence regardless of prevalence.

Macro-averaged \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_1$$\end{document} (Macro-F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document}). For each class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Prec}_k=\frac{C_{kk}}{\sum _{j}C_{jk}},\quad \textrm{Rec}_k =\frac{C_{kk}}{\sum _{j}C_{kj}},\quad F_{1,k}= \frac{2\,\textrm{Prec}_k\,\textrm{Rec}_k}{\textrm{Prec}_k+\textrm{Rec}_k}.$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm {MacroF_1}= \frac{1}{6}\sum _{k=1}^{6} F_{1,k}.$$\end{document}Weighs precision and recall equally and averages them over stages; it penalises models that ignore minority classes.

Matthews correlation coefficient (MCC).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{MCC} = \frac{\sum _{k,j} C_{kk}C_{jj}-\sum _{k,j} C_{kj}C_{jk}}{\sqrt{\Bigl (\sum _{k} r_k\Bigr )\Bigl (\sum _{j} c_j\Bigr )-\sum _{k} r_k^{\,2}}\; \sqrt{\Bigl (\sum _{k} c_k\Bigr )\Bigl (\sum _{j} r_j\Bigr )-\sum _{k} c_k^{\,2}}},$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_k=\sum _{j}C_{kj}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_k=\sum _{j}C_{jk}$$\end{document}. Yields a single chance-corrected correlation that remains interpretable under class imbalance.

Macro area under the ROC curve (Macro-AUC). For each class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k$$\end{document} a one-versus-rest ROC curve is traced using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{p_{ik}\}_{i=1}^{N}$$\end{document}; the six areas are averaged:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{MacroAUC}= \frac{1}{6}\sum _{k=1}^{6}\textrm{AUC}_k.$$\end{document}Evaluates ranking ability independent of any decision threshold.

Brier score.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Brier}= \frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^{N}\;\sum _{k=1}^{6} \bigl (p_{ik}-\mathbb {1}\{y_i=k\}\bigr )^{2}.$$\end{document}Mean-squared deviation between predicted probabilities and one-hot targets; lower values indicate better probabilistic calibration.

Accuracy and Balanced-accuracy quantify hard-label discrimination, with and without prevalence correction. Macro-F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document} rewards balanced precision-recall across stages, essential for equitable clinical triage. MCC provides a robust, chance-adjusted summary unaffected by class imbalance. Macro-AUC measures threshold-free ranking performance, relevant when operating points differ between screening and specialist contexts. Finally, the Brier score assesses probability calibration-critical because well-calibrated probabilities underpin the temperature-softening step used later in knowledge distillation. Together these six metrics furnish a comprehensive and clinically meaningful yard-stick for comparing baseline models with the proposed informed monotone network.

Statistical uncertainty quantification

All performance metrics are reported with 95% confidence intervals computed using stratified bootstrap resampling. We draw B = 1000 bootstrap samples with replacement from the 225-sample test set, preserving class proportions in each sample, and compute all metrics on each bootstrap replicate. Confidence intervals are constructed using the percentile method: the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles of the bootstrap distribution form the lower and upper bounds, respectively 29.

This approach accounts for sampling variability in the fixed test set and provides valid uncertainty estimates under mild regularity conditions. We chose 1000 iterations to ensure stable percentile estimates, following standard recommendations 29.

As seen in Table 4, among ensembles, CatBoost achieved the best discrimination and calibration (Macro-AUC 0.995; Brier 0.045); hence, we use it as the teacher in the distillation pipeline.

Teacher-student knowledge distillation

The knowledge-distillation (KD) pipeline relies on a teacher that produces well-calibrated, information-rich class probabilities. We first motivate the choice of distillation strategy, then specify the teacher configuration and calibration, and finally detail what is passed to the student during training.

Teacher-student knowledge distillation

KD methods are commonly grouped by their knowledge source (logits (transfers the teacher’s class probabilities), feature maps (forces the student to mimic intermediate feature maps), relational graphs (preserves pairwise distances or angles between samples in the teacher’s embedding space)), the transfer scheme (teacher \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow$$\end{document} student, self-distillation, mutual), and the training paradigm (offline, on-the-fly, data-free)30,31. Feature/relational KD can outperform the original logit-based method12 on large image datasets or NLP benchmarks, but they require either structurally compatible hidden activations or millions of synthetic samples; neither of which is available in our case because our dataset contains only 525 training encounters; allocating GPU memory to feature tensors or pairwise graphs would crowd out the data itself. Logit-based distillation with temperature scaling, by contrast, needs no additional memory and has outperformed heavier schemes for small, tabular, mixed-type data32,33. We therefore adopt logit KD.

Teacher model selection and calibration

Among the baselines (Table 4), CatBoost achieved the highest Macro-AUC (0.995) and lowest Brier score (0.045), indicating superior calibration. CatBoost grows oblivious (symmetric) trees and handles categorical variables via ordered target statistics34.

Raw tree probabilities can be over-confident. We calibrate with a single temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document} on a fixed 15 % split by minimising multiclass negative log-likelihood (NLL):\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^\star =\arg \min _{T>0}\;-\!\sum _{i=1}^{N_{\text {cal}}} \log \!\left( \frac{p_{i\,y_i}^{1/T}}{\sum _k p_{ik}^{1/T}}\right) .$$\end{document}The optimal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^\star =3$$\end{document} reduced NLL from 0.612 to 0.426. We also found that the expected calibration error (ECE) using 10 equal-width bins, represented as:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{ECE}=\sum _{b=1}^{10}\frac{n_b}{N}\,\bigl |\textrm{acc}(b)-\textrm{conf}(b)\bigr |,$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_b$$\end{document} is the number of samples in bin \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{acc}(b)$$\end{document} is the empirical accuracy and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{conf}(b)$$\end{document} the mean predicted confidence in that bin. After temperature scaling the ECE dropped from 4.7 % to 3.1 %, confirming that the teacher’s probabilities are well calibrated for knowledge transfer.

ECE quantifies the difference between predicted confidence and empirical accuracy across probability bins 35. Traditional ECE uses fixed-width binning (10 equal intervals in [0,1]), but this can produce unstable estimates with small sample sizes when some bins contain few or no samples 36. We compute ECE using three binning strategies: i) Fixed-width ECE (as defined above) ii) Equal-frequency ECE: Bins are defined by quantiles of the predicted confidence distribution such that each bin contains approximately n/M samples. This approach is more stable with modest sample sizes as it avoids empty or sparsely populated bins 36. We use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M=10$$\end{document} bins for aggregate ECE and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M=5$$\end{document} bins for class-wise ECE (due to smaller per-class samples). iii) Equal-mass ECE: Similar to equal-frequency but bins are weighted by the empirical confidence distribution, providing additional robustness to outliers.

Class-wise calibration

Aggregate ECE can mask class-specific miscalibration. A model may appear well-calibrated overall while being severely miscalibrated for individual classes, which is problematic for medical decision-making where different stages have different clinical implications.

For each CKD stage \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \in \{1,\dots ,6\}$$\end{document}, we compute one-vs-rest binary calibration: i) Confidence: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_i = P(y_i = k \mid \textbf{x}_i)$$\end{document} (predicted probability for stage k) ii) Correctness: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {1}[y_i = k]$$\end{document} (binary indicator) iii) Binning: Equal-frequency with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M=5$$\end{document} bins per class iv) Bootstrap: 1000 iterations for 95% confidence intervals

Brier score decomposition

The Brier score can be decomposed into three orthogonal components using the Murphy decomposition 37:3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {BS} = \text {Reliability} - \text {Resolution} + \text {Uncertainty} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where; Reliability is the average squared deviation between predicted probabilities and observed frequencies (miscalibration penalty). Lower is better; 0 indicates perfect calibration. Resolution is the ability to discriminate between outcomes by assigning different probability values (sharpness of predictions). Higher is better; captures predictive power. Uncertainty is the inherent unpredictability due to class prior distribution. This is constant for a given dataset and cannot be improved by the model.

This decomposition reveals whether Brier score differences reflect calibration issues (reliability term) or discriminative power (resolution term), providing deeper insight than the aggregate score alone. We report all components with 95% bootstrap confidence intervals (1000 iterations).

Soft-probability distillation (what the student sees)

For encounter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i$$\end{document} the teacher outputs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{p}_i=(p_{i1},\dots ,p_{i6})$$\end{document} whose largest component corresponds to the teacher’s predicted CKD stage. Before these probabilities are used as a training signal they are softened with the calibrated temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^{\star }=3$$\end{document}. We form temperature-softened targets as :\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{p}_{ik}=\frac{p_{ik}^{\,1/T^\star }}{\sum _{j=1}^{6}p_{ij}^{\,1/T^\star }},\qquad T^\star =3.$$\end{document}The exponent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1/T^\star <1$$\end{document} flattens the distribution; increasing the weight of classes, exposing the knowledge about the relationships between neighbouring CKD stages that would be lost if only the arg-maxlabel were transferred13. Because the student’s CORN head outputs probabilities directly (explored in the next section), distillation is performed in probability space. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i$$\end{document} index encounters (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1,\dots ,N$$\end{document}) and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}=\{1,\dots ,6\}$$\end{document} be the set of stages. For encounter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i$$\end{document},the teacher’s raw probability vector is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{p}_i=<p_{ik}>_{k\in \mathcal {K}}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _k p_{ik}=1$$\end{document};the softened teacher vector is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\textbf{p}}_i= <\tilde{p}_{ik} >_{k\in \mathcal {K}}$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{p}_{ik}=\frac{p_{ik}^{\,1/T^\star }}{\sum _{j\in \mathcal {K}} p_{ij}^{\,1/T^\star }},\qquad T^\star =3;$$\end{document}the student’s CORN head outputs a probability vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\textbf{p}}_i= <\hat{p}_{ik}>_{k\in \mathcal {K}}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _k \hat{p}_{ik}=1$$\end{document}.Because the student already produces probabilities, distillation is performed in probability space using the Kullback-Leibler divergence, averaged over the batch:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_{\textrm{KD}} =\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^{N}\textrm{KL}\!\bigl (\tilde{\textbf{p}}_i\,\Vert \,\hat{\textbf{p}}_i)\bigr ) =\frac{1}{N}\sum _{i=1}^{N}\sum _{k\in \mathcal {K}}\tilde{p}_{ik}\, \log \!\frac{\tilde{p}_{ik}}{\max (\hat{p}_{ik},\,10^{-8})}.$$\end{document}No additional scaling of student logits and no \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^2$$\end{document} gradient correction are required (those are only needed for logit-space KD).

the teacher’s raw probability vector is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{p}_i=<p_{ik}>_{k\in \mathcal {K}}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _k p_{ik}=1$$\end{document};

the teacher’s raw probability vector is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{p}_i=<p_{ik}>_{k\in \mathcal {K}}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _k p_{ik}=1$$\end{document};

the softened teacher vector is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\textbf{p}}_i= <\tilde{p}_{ik} >_{k\in \mathcal {K}}$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{p}_{ik}=\frac{p_{ik}^{\,1/T^\star }}{\sum _{j\in \mathcal {K}} p_{ij}^{\,1/T^\star }},\qquad T^\star =3;$$\end{document}

the softened teacher vector is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\textbf{p}}_i= <\tilde{p}_{ik} >_{k\in \mathcal {K}}$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{p}_{ik}=\frac{p_{ik}^{\,1/T^\star }}{\sum _{j\in \mathcal {K}} p_{ij}^{\,1/T^\star }},\qquad T^\star =3;$$\end{document}

the student’s CORN head outputs a probability vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\textbf{p}}_i= <\hat{p}_{ik}>_{k\in \mathcal {K}}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _k \hat{p}_{ik}=1$$\end{document}.

the student’s CORN head outputs a probability vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\textbf{p}}_i= <\hat{p}_{ik}>_{k\in \mathcal {K}}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _k \hat{p}_{ik}=1$$\end{document}.

Why heavier KD variants were not used. Feature matching (FitNets38, Attention Transfer39) presupposes convolutional/transformer backbones and inflates the student, while relational/contrastive KD scales as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(N^2)$$\end{document} in memory untenable alongside per-batch MixUp on this dataset30,40. We also trialled ordinal focal-KL and a 5-fold CatBoost ensemble; both raised validation Macro-AUC by only 0.002 at (4-5)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times$$\end{document} compute and yielded no significant test-set gain. We therefore retain single-temperature logit KD.

Student network architecture

The student is a compact, fully differentiable residual MLP that ingests the same 13 predictors as the baselines and emits (i) a probability distribution over six CKD stages and (ii) a scalar eGFR (GRF estimate). The topology satisfies the constraint of exact differentiability with respect to Scr (serum creatinine) to enable a gradient-based creatinine-monotone penalty in the final loss function.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}=\{1,\dots ,6\}$$\end{document} be the set of CKD stages. A mini-batch contains B encounters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{x}_i)_{i=1}^B$$\end{document}, each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x}_i\in \mathbb {R}^{13}$$\end{document}. The teacher is a fixed CatBoost model \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^\star {=}3$$\end{document} is the calibrated temperature. The student has width H, depth D, dropout p (disabled at evaluation), and parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document}. Algorithm 1 depicts the forward pass of the teacher softening and residual-MLP student inference.

Algorithm 1Forward pass: teacher softening and residual-MLP student inference

Forward pass: teacher softening and residual-MLP student inference

The network consists of a stem, a depth-\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D$$\end{document} residual trunk, and two task heads. All non-linearities are ReLU; dropout with rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p$$\end{document} is applied only inside the residual branch.
