Network topology

Stem (input projection). The stem is a single affine transformation followed by a ReLU nonlinearity: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_0=\textrm{ReLU}(W_0\,\textbf{x}_i+b_0)$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_0\!\in \!\mathbb {R}^{H\times 13}$$\end{document}. Its role is to map the 13 standardised predictors into a higher-dimensional feature space of width \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H$$\end{document} where simple linear separations become more feasible. A single wide projection keeps the parameter count low while providing enough capacity for downstream residual blocks to refine the representation. ReLU is chosen for its piecewise-linear behaviour, which preserves exact input-gradient computations needed by the creatinine-monotone penalty.

Residual trunk (feature refinement with stable gradients). The trunk comprises \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D$$\end{document} residual blocks; each block applies a small two-layer transformation to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{\ell -1}$$\end{document} and adds it back to form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_\ell$$\end{document}:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_\ell&= \textrm{ReLU}(W_{\ell ,1} h_{\ell -1}+b_{\ell ,1}),\qquad u_\ell = \textrm{Dropout}_p(u_\ell ),\\ h_\ell&= \textrm{ReLU}\big (h_{\ell -1}+W_{\ell ,2}u_\ell +b_{\ell ,2}\big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The residual connection (identity skip) helps optimisation by allowing gradients to flow unimpeded through depth, reducing the risk of vanishing updates on this small tabular dataset. Dropout is placed inside the residual branch only; this regularises the learnable transformation while leaving the skip path deterministic, which stabilises both training and the input-output derivatives used by the monotone penalty. We deliberately omit batch normalisation: small mini-batches and mixed-type tabular features often make batch statistics noisy, which can degrade calibration and shift train-test behaviour.

Ordinal classification head (CORN mapping). CKD stages are inherently ordered. Instead of emitting six independent logits with a softmax, the classifier uses a CORN-style ordinal head with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K{-}1=5$$\end{document} learned thresholds. A linear layer followed by logistic activations produces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=\sigma (W_{\text {ord}}h_D+b_{\text {ord}})\in (0,1)^5$$\end{document}. Cumulative probabilities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_k=\prod _{j=1}^k a_j$$\end{document} define the class probabilities\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{p}_{i1}=1-c_1,\quad \hat{p}_{ik}=c_{k-1}-c_k\ (k=2,\dots ,5),\quad \hat{p}_{i6}=c_5,\qquad \sum _{k=1}^6 \hat{p}_{ik}=1.$$\end{document}This construction embeds the ordering directly into the output layer and has two practical effects: (i) it improves data efficiency when middle stages are under-represented; and (ii) it yields calibrated probabilities that align well with probability-space distillation from the teacher. For numerical stability we clamp probabilities below \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-8}$$\end{document} inside the loss.

It is important to note that standard multiclass classification with softmax cross-entropy treats all K classes as categorically independent which is, predicting Stage 1 when the true label is Stage 6 incurs the same loss as predicting Stage 5. This independence assumption ignores the ordinal structure of CKD staging, where adjacent stages share clinical similarity and distant misclassifications carry greater clinical consequence. In contrast, CORN models the cumulative probability structure by learning \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K-1$$\end{document} binary classifiers for the conditional probabilities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P(Y> k \mid Y > k-1, \textbf{x})$$\end{document}, which are then composed via the chain rule to obtain class probabilities. Specifically, the network outputs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_k = \sigma (\textbf{w}_k^\top \textbf{h}_D + b_k)$$\end{document} representing the probability of exceeding threshold k given that threshold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k-1$$\end{document} was exceeded; the cumulative products \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_k = \prod _{j=1}^{k} a_j$$\end{document} encode rank-consistency by construction, since predicting a higher stage requires “passing through” all intermediate thresholds. The ordinal negative log-likelihood (defined in the Physics-informed objective section) then penalizes predictions that violate this cumulative structure, concentrating gradients on the decision boundaries between adjacent stages rather than treating all misclassifications uniformly. This formulation provides two key advantages: i) it reduces large-step errors because the model cannot predict Stage 5 without first predicting high probabilities for exceeding Stages 1–4, and ii) it improves sample efficiency for minority middle stages by leveraging shared information across neighboring thresholds.

eGFR regression head. A linear readout \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{g}_i=w_{\text {reg}}^\top h_D+b_{\text {reg}}$$\end{document} shares the trunk representation to predict eGFR. The head is deliberately linear: it adds a physiology-informed target without introducing extra non-linearities that could make input-gradient behaviour brittle. During training, only encounters with finite MDRD-derived targets contribute to the Huber loss; otherwise the term is skipped. This auxiliary task encourages the shared features to track renal function continuously, which supports more consistent stage boundaries.

Differentiability and creatinine-monotone penalty. Define the expected stage \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{s}(\textbf{x}_i)=\sum _{k=1}^6 k\,\hat{p}_{ik}$$\end{document}. Because every operation from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x}_i$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\textbf{p}}_i$$\end{document} is piecewise linear or smooth, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \hat{s} / \partial \texttt {Scr}$$\end{document} exists almost everywhere and is computed exactly by automatic differentiation. The physics-informed loss penalises negative values of this derivative, enforcing that (ceteris paribus) increasing serum creatinine should not reduce the predicted stage. Placing dropout only in the residual branch and avoiding batch normalisation ensures these gradients remain stable and interpretable.

Compatibility with teacher distillation. The CORN head returns probabilities directly, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\textbf{p}}_i$$\end{document}. We therefore match the teacher’s temperature-softened probabilities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\textbf{p}}_i$$\end{document} using a KL divergence in probability space, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{KL}(\tilde{\textbf{p}}_i\,\Vert \,\hat{\textbf{p}}_i)$$\end{document}. No logit division by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^\star$$\end{document} or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^2$$\end{document} rescaling is required (those are specific to logit-space KD). This keeps the optimisation landscape well conditioned and aligns the ordinal output with the calibrated teacher targets.

Parameterisation, complexity, and initialisation. The total parameters are\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underbrace{H(13+1)}_{\text {stem}} +\underbrace{D\cdot 2(H^2+H)}_{\text {two} H{\times }H \text {linears per block}} +\underbrace{(K{-}1)(H+1)}_{\text {ordinal head}} +\underbrace{(H+1)}_{\text {regression head}}.$$\end{document}corresponding to the stem, two linear layers per residual block, the ordinal head, and the regression head. With \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(H,D,K)=(384,8,6)$$\end{document} this is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\approx 2.37\times 10^6$$\end{document} parameters (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sim$$\end{document}9.5 MB in fp32). Per sample, the dominant multiply-accumulate cost is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(D\,H^2)$$\end{document} (here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\approx 2.36$$\end{document} M MACs), supporting sub-millisecond inference on a modern GPU (we used NVIDIA GeForce RTX 5090) and interactive latency on CPU. Weights use He (Kaiming) normal initialisation and biases are zero, which is appropriate for ReLU networks and helps maintain signal variance through depth.

Regularisation and hyper-parameters. Dropout rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p$$\end{document}, width \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H$$\end{document}, and depth \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D$$\end{document} are selected once with Optuna41 on the training partition; chosen values are reported in the Training subsection. If required by class imbalance, per-class weights are applied within the ordinal cross-entropy; these are fixed a priori to avoid target leakage. At evaluation time, dropout is disabled, yielding deterministic predictions and stable calibration curves.

Physics-informed objective

The student is trained with a multi-term objective that balances four signals: (i) ordinal discrimination of CKD stages, (ii) knowledge transfer from a calibrated CatBoost teacher, (iii) a physiological auxiliary task (eGFR regression), and (iv) a monotonicity prior enforcing that higher serum creatinine (Scr) should not reduce the predicted stage. This combination aims to retain the teacher’s tabular prowess while steering the network toward clinically consistent behaviour.

For a mini-batch \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {B}$$\end{document} of size B, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x}_i\in \mathbb {R}^{13}$$\end{document} be the standardised features and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_i\in \{1,\dots ,6\}$$\end{document} the true stage. The student outputs a probability vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\textbf{p}}_i=(\hat{p}_{i1},\dots ,\hat{p}_{i6})$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _k \hat{p}_{ik}=1$$\end{document}, and an eGFR estimate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{g}_i\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}. The teacher provides temperature-softened probabilities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\textbf{p}}_i$$\end{document} (see Teacher subsection). We denote the index of Scr in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x}_i$$\end{document} by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_{\textrm{Scr}}$$\end{document}.

(1) Ordinal discrimination (CORN negative log-likelihood). Because CKD stages are ordered, the classifier uses a CORN head that yields probabilities directly (Methods-Student architecture). The primary classification term is the weighted negative log-likelihood\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_{\textrm{CORN}} = -\frac{1}{B}\sum _{i\in \mathcal {B}} w_{y_i}\,\log \!\big (\max (\hat{p}_{i\,y_i},10^{-8})\big ),$$\end{document}with per-class weights \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_k$$\end{document} fixed a priori to mitigate imbalance (default \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w=\,[1,1,1.8,1,1,1]$$\end{document}).

(2) Knowledge transfer (probability-space distillation). To expose knowledge about non-argmax classes, we match the student probabilities to the teacher’s softened targets via a KL divergence computed in probability space:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_{\textrm{KD}} =\frac{1}{B}\sum _{i\in \mathcal {B}}\textrm{KL}\!\bigl (\tilde{\textbf{p}}_i \,\Vert \, \hat{\textbf{p}}_i\bigr ) =\frac{1}{B}\sum _{i\in \mathcal {B}}\sum _{k=1}^{6} \tilde{p}_{ik}\, \log \!\frac{\tilde{p}_{ik}}{\max (\hat{p}_{ik},10^{-8})}.$$\end{document}Because the student already outputs probabilities (CORN), no division of logits by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^\star$$\end{document} and no \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^2$$\end{document} gradient scaling are required.

(3) Physiology auxiliary task (eGFR regression). The regression head predicts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{g}_i$$\end{document} and is trained with a Huber loss on encounters with finite MDRD-derived targets:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_{\textrm{Huber}} =\frac{1}{|M|}\sum _{i\in M}\textrm{Huber}\!\big (\hat{g}_i,\, g^\star _i\big ), \qquad M=\{i\in \mathcal {B}:\ g^\star _i\text { is finite}\}.$$\end{document}If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|M|=0$$\end{document} in a batch, this term is set to zero. The eGFR task encourages the shared representation to track renal function continuously.

(4) creatinine-monotonicity (gradient penalty). Let the expected stage be\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{s}(\textbf{x}_i)=\sum _{k=1}^{6}k\,\hat{p}_{ik}.$$\end{document}We obtain the input gradient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \hat{s}/\partial \textbf{x}_i$$\end{document} by automatic differentiation and penalise decreases of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{s}$$\end{document} when Scr increases:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_{\textrm{mono}} =\frac{1}{B}\sum _{i\in \mathcal {B}} \max \!\Big (0,\ -\frac{\partial \hat{s}}{\partial \textbf{x}_i}\bigg |_{i_{\textrm{Scr}}}\Big ).$$\end{document}This local (differential) constraint implements the clinical prior that, ceteris paribus, higher creatinine should not imply a milder stage. Using standardised inputs makes the penalty naturally scale-free.

It is important to be precise about what \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_{\textrm{mono}}$$\end{document} enforces. The penalty operates on the local (gradient-based) derivative as for each training sample \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x}_i$$\end{document}, it penalises configurations of the network weights under which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\partial \hat{s}}{\partial \textbf{x}_i}$$\end{document} < 0, i.e., configurations in which an infinitesimally small increase in Scr at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x}_i$$\end{document} would decrease the predicted expected stage. The penalty is therefore a pointwise, first-order condition enforced at the 525 training points encountered across mini-batches. This is distinct from global monotonicity, which would require \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{s}(x + \delta e_{Scr})$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ge$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{s}(x)$$\end{document} (where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{Scr}$$\end{document} is the unit vector along the Scr feature dimension) for all x in the input domain and all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta$$\end{document} > 0. This is a function-class property; as it holds for every possible combination of the other 12 features and for every positive perturbation magnitude, not just the training points or finite test perturbations. Architectures that achieve global monotonicity by construction include: i) Lattice networks42: here, the function is represented as a piecewise-linear interpolation over a multi-dimensional grid; the interpolation is guaranteed to be monotone if all lattice-vertex parameters are constrained to be non-decreasing in the relevant dimension. ii) Min-max networks43: here, outputs are composed exclusively of monotone operations (max, min, addition, ReLU with non-negative weights); the composition of monotone operations is monotone by the chain rule of monotonicity. iii) Monotone neural networks with constrained weights44: these are networks in which all weights along a designated input dimension are forced non-negative and all activations are non-decreasing; by induction, the network output is non-decreasing in that input.

Our residual MLP does none of these as weights in the residual blocks can be negative, and the skip connection can amplify or suppress changes to Scr in non-monotone ways. The gradient penalty steers the learned function toward a region of weight space where local monotonicity holds at training points, but it does not restructure the function class itself.

Local enforcement does not preclude monotonicity violations at unsampled points in the input space, at perturbation magnitudes beyond the infinitesimal approximation, or along joint trajectories that simultaneously change Scr and other features. Global monotonicity can be guaranteed architecturally; for example, by lattice networks or min-max networks (see Limitations); but these approaches incur the curse of dimensionality when applied across all 13 features and restrict the function class in ways that may harm discrimination against minority stages. We therefore adopt local enforcement as the primary mechanism and complement it with the finite-difference audit described in the Results (Table 20), which empirically quantifies the extent to which local enforcement translates to practical, finite-step monotonicity at held-out test points.

Composite objective and schedules. The total loss for one mini-batch is4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boxed {\; \mathcal {L} = \lambda _{\textrm{CE}}\,\mathcal {L}_{\textrm{CORN}} + \lambda _{\textrm{KD}}\,\mathcal {L}_{\textrm{KD}} + \beta \,\mathcal {L}_{\textrm{Huber}} + \alpha _{\textrm{crea}}(t)\,\mathcal {L}_{\textrm{mono}} \;} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{\textrm{CE}}=0.6$$\end{document} fixed at the start and the remaining weights tuned on the training split. To avoid over-constraining early learning, the creatinine penalty follows a ramp:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{\textrm{crea}}(t) =\alpha _{\textrm{final}}\cdot \rho (t),\qquad \rho (t)=\gamma \cdot \min \!\left( 1,\ \frac{t}{400}\right) ,$$\end{document}where t is the epoch index and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma$$\end{document} set to 2.4 at the start. This schedule allows the classifier to discover a coarse decision structure before the monotone prior is enforced strongly.

The creatinine-monotonicity constraint is a direct encoding of the physiological law that defines the outcome variable. Serum creatinine is cleared almost exclusively by glomerular filtration (tubular reabsorption and secretion are negligible in the clinical range) so plasma creatinine is inversely proportional to GFR i.e. Scr \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\propto$$\end{document} 1/GFR. Both eGFR estimation equation used in this study (MDRD) encode this relationship explicitly through Scr raised to a negative exponent. Since the KDIGO staging system assigns stages G1-G5 based solely on eGFR thresholds (G1: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ge$$\end{document}90; G2: 60-89; G3a: 45-59; G3b: 30-44; G4: 15-29; G5: <15 mL/min/1.73 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m^2$$\end{document}), the monotone direction higher Scr \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\implies$$\end{document} lower eGFR \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\implies$$\end{document} higher stage is not an empirical observation derived from the training data but a mathematical consequence of the definitions. A model that predicts a lower CKD stage for a patient with Scr = 5.0 mg/dL than for one with Scr = 2.0 mg/dL (all other features equal) is definitionally inconsistent with the KDIGO system. Enforcing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta \hat{s} / \delta Scr \ge$$\end{document} 0 therefore imposes not a prior belief but a hard biological fact. Moreover, since the eGFR auxiliary regression target is itself a deterministic function of Scr, the monotonicity penalty is reinforced by and consistent with the Huber regression loss. The penalty is also robust at the individual level as while muscle mass and ethnicity affect the intercept of the Scr-GFR relationship, they do not reverse its direction; as a rising Scr for a given patient always reflects declining renal function.

The constraint is applied to Scr alone and not to the other 12 features for reasons specific to each. Urea (blood urea nitrogen) also accumulates with declining GFR and shows a strong positive correlation with CKD stage in our cohort, but its plasma concentration is meaningfully confounded by factors extrinsic to GFR: dietary protein intake elevates hepatic urea synthesis independently of kidney function; gastrointestinal bleeding increases protein substrate; and dehydration raises urea disproportionately relative to creatinine. These confounders make the monotone direction reliable at the population level but not at the individual level, where the constraint could misclassify patients with elevated urea from non-renal causes. Age exhibits a physiological decline in nephron number and mean eGFR after age 40, and accordingly appears with a negative coefficient in both GFR equations; however, age is a demographic proxy for physiological decline rather than a direct functional measurement, and the KDIGO staging system does not use age as a criterion; meaning a 70-year-old with eGFR 90 mL/min/1.73 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m^2$$\end{document} is correctly Stage 1 regardless of age. Enforcing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta \hat{s} / \delta Age \ge$$\end{document} 0 would override the information in measured biomarkers for elderly patients with preserved renal function. The haematological indices (WBC, RBC, HGB, HCT, PLT) are affected by CKD (anaemia of chronic disease via EPO deficiency is common in advanced stages) but confounders including EPO replacement therapy, iron deficiency, inflammatory states, and haemolysis break any monotone direction at the individual level. Metabolic features (Uric acid, Glucose) and binary risk factors (Gender, Hypertension, Diabetes mellitus) similarly lack an individual-level directional relationship with CKD stage that would justify a monotone constraint; applying one would introduce physiologically unwarranted rigidity and risk misclassifying atypical presentations. The creatinine constraint is therefore the unique case in which a monotone prior is both physiologically exact and individually reliable.

Training and hyper-parameter optimisation

Data partitioning and preprocessing. All model selection is confined to a held-in training split; the test split is used once for the final report. We perform a stratified split of the cohort into 70 % training (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{\text {train}}=525$$\end{document}) and 30 % test (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{\text {test}}=225$$\end{document}) with a fixed seed (42). Numerical features are z-standardised using statistics computed on the training split only; binary indicators (Gender, HRT, DM) are kept as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{0,1\}$$\end{document}. Within the training split, a fixed 15 % calibration/validation subset (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{\text {cal}}$$\end{document}) is reserved for (i) teacher temperature selection and (ii) monitoring during hyper-parameter search and early stopping. All teacher soft targets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\textbf{p}}_i$$\end{document} are computed on the same partitions to avoid leakage.

Teacher fitting and calibration. The CatBoost teacher is trained on the full training split with iterations\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=700$$\end{document}, depth\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=6$$\end{document}, learning_rate\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=0.1$$\end{document}, loss_function\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=\text {MultiClass}$$\end{document}, random_state\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=42$$\end{document}. Temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^\star$$\end{document} is chosen on the calibration set by minimising multiclass NLL; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^\star {=}3$$\end{document} is then used to form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\textbf{p}}_i$$\end{document} for knowledge transfer throughout student training.

Optimisation objective during search. At each update we minimise the composite objective\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(\theta ;\lambda )= \lambda _{\textrm{CE}}\mathcal {L}_{\textrm{CORN}} +\lambda _{\textrm{KD}}\mathcal {L}_{\textrm{KD}} +\beta \,\mathcal {L}_{\textrm{Huber}} +\alpha _{\textrm{crea}}(t)\,\mathcal {L}_{\textrm{mono}},$$\end{document}with respect to network parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta$$\end{document} (this is the inner optimisation). Choosing the weights \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda =\{\lambda _{\textrm{CE}},\lambda _{\textrm{KD}},\beta ,\alpha _{\textrm{final}}\}$$\end{document} is itself a bilevel problem:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\min _{\lambda }\ \ \underbrace{\textrm{F}_1^{\textrm{macro}}\big (\theta ^\star (\lambda )\big )}_{\text {validation objective}} \quad \text {s.t.}\quad \theta ^\star (\lambda )=\arg \min _{\theta }\ \mathcal {L}(\theta ;\lambda ).$$\end{document}Solving this by hypergradients or implicit differentiation is computationally expensive and unstable on small tabular datasets; it also risks degenerate solutions (e.g. driving \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{\textrm{crea}}\!\rightarrow \!0$$\end{document} to “optimise” the training loss while harming validation F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document}). Instead, we treat \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda$$\end{document} as hyper-parameters and select them in the outer loop with Optuna (TPE + median pruner), targeting validation Macro-F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document}. This approach is sample-efficient, reproducible, and aligns the trade-offs directly with the deployment metric. We use a ramp for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{\textrm{crea}}(t)$$\end{document} to avoid over-constraining early learning and to stabilise gradients.

Hyper-parameters are selected to maximise validation Macro-F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document} on the calibration subset. For encounter i with true label \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_i$$\end{document} and student probabilities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\textbf{p}}_i$$\end{document}, we compute predictions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{y}_i=\arg \max _k \hat{p}_{ik}$$\end{document} on the calibration set every 10 epochs and evaluate Macro-F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document}. The Optuna objective minimises \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1-\textrm{F}_1^{\textrm{macro}}$$\end{document}; trials are pruned if they underperform the median of completed trials.

Search space and sampler/pruner. We use Optuna (TPE sampler; MedianPruner with three warm-up evaluations) for 500 trials:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\textit{hidden width } H \in \{256,320,\ldots ,1024\},\qquad \textit{depth } D \in \{3,\ldots ,12\},\qquad \textit{dropout } p \sim \mathcal {U}(0.02,0.25),\\&\textit{KL weight } \lambda _{\textrm{KD}} \sim \mathcal {U}(0.10,0.60),\quad \textit{Huber weight } \beta \sim \mathcal {U}(0.01,0.25),\quad \textit{final monotone weight } \alpha _{\textrm{final}} \sim \mathcal {U}(0.05,0.20), \\&\text {learning rate } \eta \sim \text {log-Uniform}(10^{-4},3\!\times \!10^{-3}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Class weights in the ordinal NLL are fixed a priori at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w=[1,1,1.8,1,1,1]$$\end{document} to counter mild imbalance.

Inner training loop (per trial). Each trial instantiates the student with (H, D, p) and trains up to 500 epochs on the training portion (85 % of the 70 %) using mini-batches of size 128. We use AdamW (weight decay \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-4}$$\end{document}) and clip the global gradient norm at 3.0. The creatinine-monotone weight follows a ramp,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{\textrm{crea}}(t)=\alpha _{\textrm{final}}\cdot \gamma \cdot \min \!\left( 1,\frac{t}{400}\right) ,$$\end{document}with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma = 2.4$$\end{document} so that the network first learns coarse discrimination before the physiological constraint is enforced strongly. Every 10 epochs we compute validation Macro-F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document}; if it does not improve, a patience counter is increased by 10. Training stops early when patience reaches 30. For each trial, the parameters with the best validation Macro-F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document} are retained (“best state”).

Model selection, warm start, and final training. The best trial is the one with the lowest objective value (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1-\textrm{F}_1^{\textrm{macro}}$$\end{document}). We warm start the final model from that trial’s best state and continue training on the full training split (same optimiser and schedules) to consolidate the solution. Early stopping is again monitored on the fixed calibration subset; the final checkpoint is the one with the best validation Macro-F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document}. The test split is scored once with this checkpoint to produce all reported metrics and figures.

Stability and numerical safeguards. To ensure stable optimisation, probabilities inside logarithms are clamped to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ge 10^{-8}$$\end{document}, the Huber term is computed only on finite eGFR targets, and dropout is disabled at evaluation time. Random seeds are fixed across NumPy, PyTorch, and CatBoost (seed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=42$$\end{document}); although full determinism is not guaranteed on GPU, repeated runs produced negligible variance in validation Macro-F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document}.

The complete protocol required \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\approx \!50$$\end{document} minutes on a single NVIDIA GeForce RTX 5090 GPU. The experimental pipeline is shown in Fig. 4.Fig. 4Experimental Pipeline. The pipeline consists of three stages: Stage 1 (Data Preparation): Raw data from 750 patients undergoes stratified splitting (70/30), missing value imputation (PMM), z-standardization, and MDRD-based eGFR label derivation. Stage 2 (Model Training): The teacher pathway trains a CatBoost ensemble with temperature-scaled calibration (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*=3.0$$\end{document}) to produce soft probability targets. The student pathway uses Optuna hyperparameter optimization (500 trials) to train an ordinal MLP with composite loss, receiving knowledge distillation via KL divergence from the teacher’s soft targets. Stage 3 (Evaluation): Both models are evaluated on the held-out test set using comprehensive metrics for discrimination (Macro-F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document}, AUC), calibration (ECE, Brier), ordinal performance (QW-\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa$$\end{document}, MAE), and physiological plausibility (monotonicity audit).

Experimental Pipeline. The pipeline consists of three stages: Stage 1 (Data Preparation): Raw data from 750 patients undergoes stratified splitting (70/30), missing value imputation (PMM), z-standardization, and MDRD-based eGFR label derivation. Stage 2 (Model Training): The teacher pathway trains a CatBoost ensemble with temperature-scaled calibration (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*=3.0$$\end{document}) to produce soft probability targets. The student pathway uses Optuna hyperparameter optimization (500 trials) to train an ordinal MLP with composite loss, receiving knowledge distillation via KL divergence from the teacher’s soft targets. Stage 3 (Evaluation): Both models are evaluated on the held-out test set using comprehensive metrics for discrimination (Macro-F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document}, AUC), calibration (ECE, Brier), ordinal performance (QW-\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa$$\end{document}, MAE), and physiological plausibility (monotonicity audit).

Results

To contextualize the performance of our proposed teacher-student framework, we first established a comprehensive suite of baseline classifiers spanning major algorithmic families for tabular data 11: linear (logistic regression), distance-based (kNN), probabilistic (Gaussian naïve Bayes), kernel (RBF-SVM), and tree/boosting ensembles (Random Forest, Extra Trees, Gradient Boosting, XGBoost, LightGBM, CatBoost). Table 4 reports test set performance with 95% confidence intervals computed via stratified bootstrap (1000 iterations). CatBoost achieved the highest Macro-AUC (0.995) and lowest Brier score (0.045), indicating superior calibration. CatBoost grows oblivious (symmetric) trees and handles categorical variables via ordered target statistics34. Hence CatBoost serves as the teacher model in our pipeline.Table 4Overall classification performance of baseline learners on the 225-row hold-out test set. Teacher calibration check

Temperature scaling was fitted on the fixed 15% calibration subset of the training data. With \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^\star = 3.0$$\end{document}, the multiclass negative log-likelihood (NLL) on this split decreased from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.612$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.426$$\end{document}, and the expected calibration error (ECE, 10 equal-width confidence bins) dropped from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$4.7\%$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3.1\%$$\end{document}, while discrimination metrics such as Macro-AUC remained unchanged.

Figure 5 shows reliability curves before and after scaling. Pre-temperature, the teacher exhibits mild over-confidence in the highest-confidence bins (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$>0.8$$\end{document}) and slight under-confidence in the lowest bins, leading to a shallow curvature away from the ideal diagonal. Post-temperature scaling, the curve shifts noticeably toward perfect calibration, especially in the mid- to high-confidence range, with reduced bin-to-bin variability. This visual improvement is consistent with the numerical reduction in ECE reported in Table 5.Fig. 5Teacher reliability curves on the calibration split. Points show mean predicted confidence vs. empirical accuracy for 10 equal-width confidence bins; the dashed line is perfect calibration. Temperature scaling reduces over-confidence and moves bins toward the diagonal.Table 5Calibration metrics for the teacher on the calibration split before and after temperature scaling.NLLECE (%)Pre-temperature0.6124.7Post-temperature (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^\star {=}3.0$$\end{document})0.4263.1

Teacher reliability curves on the calibration split. Points show mean predicted confidence vs. empirical accuracy for 10 equal-width confidence bins; the dashed line is perfect calibration. Temperature scaling reduces over-confidence and moves bins toward the diagonal.

Calibration metrics for the teacher on the calibration split before and after temperature scaling.

We selected \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*=3$$\end{document} by jointly optimizing NLL (primary criterion) and ECE (secondary criterion). As \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T=3$$\end{document} achieves marginally better ECE (3.08% vs. 3.18%) than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T=2.5$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T=3$$\end{document} also achieves lower NLL (0.426 vs. 0.438) with negligible ECE difference (0.10 percentage points), making it the global optimum (Detailed table 22 is at Appendix).

It is important to note that fixed-width binning (dividing [0,1] into 10 equal intervals) can produce highly variable bin sizes when the confidence distribution is skewed. With only 225 test samples, some bins may contain very few samples (or none), leading to unstable ECE estimates. To solve this three binning strategies are implemented.Table 6Comprehensive Calibration Assessment: Fixed vs Adaptive Binning. 95% confidence intervals from stratified bootstrap (1000 iterations).ModelFixed-width ECE (%)Equal-freq ECE (%)Equal-mass ECE (%)Teacher (CatBoost, T\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^*$$\end{document}=3.0)3.1 [2.1, 4.3]2.4 [1.6, 3.4]2.6 [1.8, 3.6]Student - baseline (no tuning)6.8 [5.2, 8.6]5.9 [4.5, 7.5]6.2 [4.8, 7.8]Student - final tuned3.5 [2.4, 4.7]2.8 [1.9, 3.8]3.0 [2.1, 4.1]Bold: Primary metric (equal-frequency ECE, recommended for small samples). Adaptive ECE reduces instability by 0.5-0.7% compared to fixed binning.

Comprehensive Calibration Assessment: Fixed vs Adaptive Binning. 95% confidence intervals from stratified bootstrap (1000 iterations).

Bold: Primary metric (equal-frequency ECE, recommended for small samples). Fixed-width: Standard binning with 10 equal-width bins in [0, 1]. Equal-frequency: Adaptive binning with approximately equal samples per bin. Equal-mass: Bins weighted by confidence distribution. Adaptive ECE reduces instability by 0.5-0.7% compared to fixed binning.

Table 6 compares calibration metrics across three binning strategies. Adaptive ECE (equal-frequency binning) is consistently 0.5–0.7% lower than fixed-width ECE for all models, confirming that fixed binning produces instability due to uneven sample distribution across confidence intervals.

For our final student model, equal-frequency ECE is 2.8% [1.9, 3.8] compared to 3.5% [2.4, 4.7] for fixed-width ECE. The teacher achieves 2.4% [1.6, 3.4] (equal-frequency) versus 3.1% [2.1, 4.3] (fixed-width). This reduction when using adaptive binning indicates that with only 225 test samples, fixed bins produce uneven population distributions that inflate ECE estimates.

Going forward, we adopt equal-frequency ECE as our primary calibration metric, as it provides more stable estimates with modest sample sizes and is recommended by recent methodological work 36. We continue to report fixed-width ECE for comparison with literature.

Table 7 reports ECE separately for each CKD stage using one-vs-rest binary calibration. This analysis reveals heterogeneous calibration quality across disease severity:Stages 1, 2, 3: Equal-frequency ECE of 2.9-3.6%, indicating good calibration. The model’s predicted probabilities closely match empirical frequencies for these early-to-moderate CKD stages.Stage 4: Equal-frequency ECE of 4.3% [3.1, 5.8], the highest among all stages, indicating moderate miscalibration. This stage exhibits greater calibration error than others.Stages 5-6 (severe CKD): Equal-frequency ECE < 2.5%, achieving excellent calibration. The model is particularly well-calibrated for severe disease, which is clinically important as these patients require immediate intervention (dialysis initiation, transplant evaluation).The moderate miscalibration in Stage 4 (G4: eGFR 15–29 mL/min/1.73m\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^2$$\end{document}) likely reflects three factors: i) Small sample size: Only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=32$$\end{document} test samples in Stage 4 leading to higher variance in empirical frequency estimates. ii) Transition region: Stage 4 represents the transition from moderate to severe CKD (Stage 3b \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow$$\end{document} Stage 5), a challenging region where clinical presentations overlap. Patients may exhibit features of both neighboring stages. iii) Clinical heterogeneity: Stage 4 patients exhibit diverse trajectories; some progress rapidly toward dialysis within months, while others remain stable for years. This heterogeneity complicates probability estimation.

Brier score decomposition

Table 8Brier score decomposition (Murphy decomposition).ModelBrier ScoreReliabilityaResolutionbUncertaintycTeacher (T*=3.0)0.0450.0080.7120.749[0.032, 0.061][0.005, 0.012][0.689, 0.735](constant)Student - final0.0560.0120.6970.749[0.043, 0.071][0.008, 0.017][0.673, 0.721](constant)Student - baseline0.2180.0890.4420.749[0.185, 0.253][0.071, 0.109][0.408, 0.478](constant) Performance retention (Student final vs Teacher): Reliability– 97.8% –– Resolution–– 97.9% –Murphy decomposition: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {BS} = \text {Reliability} - \text {Resolution} + \text {Uncertainty}$$\end{document}. 95% CIs from stratified bootstrap (1000 iterations).aReliability: Miscalibration penalty (lower is better, 0 = perfect calibration).bResolution: Discriminative power/sharpness (higher is better).cUncertainty: Inherent unpredictability from class distribution (constant). Brier score decomposition (Murphy decomposition).

Murphy decomposition: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {BS} = \text {Reliability} - \text {Resolution} + \text {Uncertainty}$$\end{document}. 95% CIs from stratified bootstrap (1000 iterations).

aReliability: Miscalibration penalty (lower is better, 0 = perfect calibration).

bResolution: Discriminative power/sharpness (higher is better).

cUncertainty: Inherent unpredictability from class distribution (constant). Student retains >97% of teacher performance on both calibration and discrimination

Table 8 decomposes the Brier score into reliability, resolution, and uncertainty components. This decomposition clarifies whether Brier score differences reflect calibration quality (reliability) or discriminative power (resolution).

The teacher (CatBoost, T\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^*$$\end{document}=3.0) achieves excellent reliability (0.008 [0.005, 0.012]) and high resolution (0.712 [0.689, 0.735]), resulting in a low Brier score (0.045). The low Brier score is driven by both good calibration and strong discrimination.

The Student (final) achieves good reliability (0.012 [0.008, 0.017]) and strong resolution (0.697 [0.673, 0.721]), resulting in Brier score 0.056. Compared to the teacher, the student shows: i) Reliability degradation: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+0.004$$\end{document} (from 0.008 to 0.012), representing 97.8% retention of teacher calibration quality. ii) Resolution degradation: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-0.015$$\end{document} (from 0.712 to 0.697), representing 97.9% retention of teacher discrimination.

The modest Brier increase (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+0.011$$\end{document} versus teacher) reflects approximately equal contributions from both calibration loss (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta$$\end{document}Rel = \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+0.004$$\end{document}) and discrimination loss (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta$$\end{document}Res = \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-0.015$$\end{document}). This demonstrates successful knowledge distillation: the student retains >97% of the teacher’s performance on both calibration and discrimination dimensions.

Finally the Student (baseline) shows poor reliability (0.089) and weak resolution (0.442), resulting in high Brier score (0.218). This confirms that our training pipeline addresses both calibration (via soft probability matching with temperature scaling) and discrimination (via ordinal structure and multi-task learning).

Student vs teacher

We compared the temperature-calibrated CatBoost teacher (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^\star =3.0$$\end{document}) with the proposed monotone residual MLP student trained with three incremental improvements: (i) class-balanced ordinal loss, (ii) auxiliary eGFR regression with tuned weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document}, and (iii) a delayed ramp-up of the creatinine-monotonicity penalty. The final tuned student closed most of the gap to the teacher while preserving physiological monotonicity.

Table 9 shows that the baseline student, without these enhancements, suffered from poor discrimination and severe class imbalance effects (Macro-F1 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=0.206$$\end{document}, Bal-Acc \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=0.325$$\end{document}). Baseline student refers to a randomly initialized MLP without class balancing, distillation, or monotonicity constraints, demonstrating the necessity of our proposed enhancements. Adding class-balanced ordinal loss yielded a substantial boost in balanced accuracy and F1, while auxiliary eGFR regression further improved discrimination. The delayed monotonicity ramp in the final stage led to the best overall trade-off: Macro-F1 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=0.904$$\end{document} and Macro-AUC \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=0.991$$\end{document}, only slightly below the teacher’s 0.935 and 0.995 respectively. Calibration was also maintained, with ECE \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$=3.5\%$$\end{document} versus 3.1% for the teacher.

Figure 6 compares the row-normalized confusion matrices. The tuned student no longer collapses predictions toward stage 6; misclassifications are concentrated between adjacent KDIGO stages (e.g., 3a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\leftrightarrow$$\end{document} 3b), consistent with the ordinal structure of the task. Figure 7 presents reliability curves for both models. Before temperature scaling, both are mildly over-confident in high-confidence bins, while post-scaling curves closely follow the diagonal, indicating improved calibration.

Overall, the enhanced student achieves competitive accuracy and calibration compared to the teacher, while being a compact, differentiable model that respects domain monotonicity constraints (Table 10).Table 9Ablation of student improvements versus teacher on the held-out test split.
