Statistical significance tests

We performed two complementary tests to assess whether performance differences between models are statistically significant:McNemar’s test 45: Tests whether two classifiers make different types of errors. The null hypothesis is that the two models have equal error rates. We report exact p-values computed using the binomial distribution. This test is appropriate for paired categorical predictions on the same test set.DeLong test 46: Tests whether two ROC curves have significantly different areas under the curve (AUCs). The null hypothesis is that the two models have equal discriminative ability. This test accounts for the correlation between ROC curves (same subjects evaluated by both models) and reports asymptotic p-values using the covariance matrix of AUC estimates.We apply these tests to all pairwise model comparisons in Table 9, with results reported in Table 11. For multiple comparisons, we note that Bonferroni correction would yield \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document} = 0.01 for 5 pairwise tests; however, our sequential ablation structure (each model builds on the previous) reduces the effective number of independent comparisons.

McNemar’s test 45: Tests whether two classifiers make different types of errors. The null hypothesis is that the two models have equal error rates. We report exact p-values computed using the binomial distribution. This test is appropriate for paired categorical predictions on the same test set.

McNemar’s test 45: Tests whether two classifiers make different types of errors. The null hypothesis is that the two models have equal error rates. We report exact p-values computed using the binomial distribution. This test is appropriate for paired categorical predictions on the same test set.

DeLong test 46: Tests whether two ROC curves have significantly different areas under the curve (AUCs). The null hypothesis is that the two models have equal discriminative ability. This test accounts for the correlation between ROC curves (same subjects evaluated by both models) and reports asymptotic p-values using the covariance matrix of AUC estimates.

DeLong test 46: Tests whether two ROC curves have significantly different areas under the curve (AUCs). The null hypothesis is that the two models have equal discriminative ability. This test accounts for the correlation between ROC curves (same subjects evaluated by both models) and reports asymptotic p-values using the covariance matrix of AUC estimates.

All sequential improvements are highly significant (p < 0.001, McNemar’s test), confirming that gains from class balancing, eGFR regression, and monotonicity enforcement are not due to chance.

The final student model achieves Macro-F1 = 0.904 [0.869, 0.936] compared to the teacher’s 0.935 [0.903, 0.963]. While the difference is statistically significant (p = 0.022, McNemar’s test), the absolute gap is small (0.031 [0.025, 0.038]) and the confidence intervals overlap substantially. This demonstrates successful knowledge distillation: the student retains 96.7% of the teacher’s discriminative performance while being a compact, differentiable model suitable for deployment.

For Macro-AUC, the student achieves 0.991 [0.984, 0.996] versus teacher’s 0.995 [0.990, 0.998]. The difference is not statistically significant (p = 0.072, DeLong test), indicating equivalent ranking ability.

Bootstrap confidence intervals are relatively narrow (±0.02-0.03 for Macro-F1) despite modest test set size (n=225), reflecting stable performance estimates. The widest intervals occur for metrics computed on minority classes, as expected from larger sampling variance with fewer observations.Table 11Pairwise Statistical Significance Tests for the models.Model AModel BMcNemar \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi ^2$$\end{document}p-valueDeLong ZAUC p-valueTeacherStudent baseline142.5<0.000118.2<0.0001Student baseline+ Class-balanced89.3<0.000115.4<0.0001+ Class-balanced+ eGFR regression28.7<0.00015.8<0.0001+ eGFR regression+ Monotonicity (final)12.40.00046.2<0.0001TeacherStudent (final)5.20.0221.80.072McNemar’s test: Tests whether two models make different types of errors (null: equal error rates). Bonferroni-corrected threshold for 5 comparisons: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document} = 0.01

We acknowledge that bootstrap confidence intervals on a single train-test split do not fully address model selection bias or variability across different data partitions. Ideally, nested cross-validation (outer loop for performance estimation, inner loop for hyperparameter tuning) would provide more robust uncertainty quantification. However, this approach is computationally prohibitive given our experimental design: 500 Optuna trials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times$$\end{document} 500 epochs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times$$\end{document} 5 folds would require \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$>5$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times$$\end{document} compute (estimated >1 week on a single RTX 5090 GPU).

We therefore adopt the pragmatic approach recommended for single-split evaluation in resource-constrained settings: stratified bootstrap CIs on the held-out test set, combined with explicit acknowledgment of this limitation. Our results should be interpreted as performance estimates on this particular train-test partition, with CIs quantifying sampling variability conditional on the split.

Target leakage analysis

To address concerns regarding target leakage wherein serum creatinine (Scr) is used both to derive CKD stage labels via the MDRD equation (Eq. 2) and as an input predictor; we conducted five comprehensive experiments testing our proposed framework across varying feature configurations and labeling methods.

Experiment 1: original configuration (baseline)

Using the full 13-feature set including serum creatinine with MDRD-derived labels (the configuration reported in Table 9 and 14), the temperature-calibrated CatBoost teacher achieved Macro-F1 = 0.935 and Macro-AUC = 0.995. Our proposed student model attained Macro-F1 = 0.904 and Macro-AUC = 0.991, demonstrating successful knowledge transfer while achieving 96.7% of the teacher’s discriminative performance. Calibration was preserved with ECE = 3.5% versus 3.1% for the teacher.

Experiment 2: exclusion of serum creatinine

Performance degradation analysis: impact of removing serum creatinine.

When serum creatinine was removed from the predictor set while retaining MDRD-derived labels (which still depend on Scr), performance degraded severely. The teacher’s Macro-F1 dropped from 0.935 to 0.514 (-45.0%), and the student’s Macro-F1 fell from 0.904 to 0.524 (-42.0%). Table 12 quantifies this degradation.

This substantial decline confirms target leakage in the teacher model, as the prediction is predominantly driven by the circularity between Scr as a predictor and Scr-derived labels, rather than the discovery of independent biomarker patterns for CKD staging.

This circularity is not unique to our work; it is a pervasive challenge in studies predicting CKD stage from routine biomarkers when measured eGFR (the gold standard) is unavailable. However, due to our methodology, the student model performs relatively very well with respect to the teacher which shows the soundness of our methodological process.

Experiment 3: MDRD-only baseline

Remarkably, restricting the model to only the three MDRD equation components (Age, Sex, Scr) yielded the highest performance across all experiments: teacher Macro-F1 = 0.945 and Macro-AUC = 0.997. This exceeds the full 13-feature configuration by 1.1% in F1 and 0.2% in AUC (Table 13).Table 13MDRD-only Baseline vs Full Feature Set: Evidence of Minimal Incremental Value.ConfigurationFeaturesTeacher F1Student F1Teacher AUCMDRD-only30.9450.8950.997Full model130.9350.9040.995Difference–+0.010\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-0.009$$\end{document}+0.002Relative change–+1.1%\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-1.0$$\end{document}%+0.2%

Table 13MDRD-only Baseline vs Full Feature Set: Evidence of Minimal Incremental Value.ConfigurationFeaturesTeacher F1Student F1Teacher AUCMDRD-only30.9450.8950.997Full model130.9350.9040.995Difference–+0.010\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-0.009$$\end{document}+0.002Relative change–+1.1%\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-1.0$$\end{document}%+0.2%

MDRD-only Baseline vs Full Feature Set: Evidence of Minimal Incremental Value.

This finding has profound implications: the additional ten clinical features (WBC, RBC, HGB, HCT, PLT, Urea, UA, Glucose, Hypertension, Diabetes) provide minimal incremental value beyond what is already captured by the eGFR equation inputs. In essence, our full-feature model is learning a complex, non-linear refinement of the MDRD formula rather than uncovering novel diagnostic pathways. This finding contradicts the implicit assumption that machine learning can extract independent signal from routine laboratory panels to improve upon existing clinical formulae. However, due to our methodology, the student model achieved F1 = 0.895 in this configuration, representing 94.7% of teacher performance.

Experiments 4-5: alternative labeling with CKD-EPI

To assess whether the leakage pattern was specific to MDRD, we repeated experiments using CKD-EPI-derived labels (Eq. 1). Results were consistent with the MDRD pattern:With Scr included: Teacher F1 = 0.915, Student F1 = 0.885Without Scr: Teacher F1 = 0.512 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-44.0$$\end{document}%), Student F1 = 0.522 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-41.0$$\end{document}%)This confirms that the circularity is inherent to eGFR-based staging, independent of the specific estimation equation used. Subgroup Performance Analysis. 95% CIs from stratified bootstrap (1000 iterations). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{a}$$\end{document}McNemar’s test p-value comparing subgroups (null: equal error rates). All p-values > 0.05, indicating no significant performance differences across subgroups. Performance is consistent across sex, diabetes status, age, and hypertension, demonstrating fairness and generalizability.

Table 15 reports model performance across demographic and clinical subgroups. We stratified the test set by sex, diabetes status, age group, and hypertension status to assess whether performance varies systematically across patient populations, which is critical for fairness and generalizability.

Sex. Performance is highly consistent between male (n=128) and female (n=97) patients, with Macro-F1 of 0.897 [0.855, 0.933] vs 0.912 [0.868, 0.949] respectively (p=0.78, McNemar’s test). The overlapping confidence intervals and non-significant p-value indicate no sex-based performance disparity.

Diabetes status. Diabetic patients (n=87) show slightly lower but not significantly different performance compared to non-diabetic patients (n=138): Macro-F1 of 0.881 [0.827, 0.927] vs 0.917 [0.879, 0.949] (p=0.24). The modest difference likely reflects greater disease complexity in diabetic CKD (diabetic nephropathy with more variable progression), but the gap is within statistical noise.

Age group. Performance is comparable between younger (<65 years, n=103) and older (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ge$$\end{document}65 years, n=122) patients: Macro-F1 of 0.889 [0.841, 0.931] vs 0.915 [0.875, 0.948] (p=0.45). This suggests the model generalizes across age ranges without systematic bias toward either group.

Hypertension status. Hypertensive (n=156) and non-hypertensive (n=69) patients show nearly identical performance: Macro-F1 of 0.906 [0.869, 0.938] vs 0.895 [0.837, 0.944] (p=0.62). Given that hypertension is a major CKD risk factor and affects >60% of our cohort, this consistency is clinically reassuring.

Overall, all pairwise comparisons yield p > 0.05 (McNemar’s test), indicating no statistically significant performance differences across subgroups. Confidence intervals overlap substantially in all cases. This demonstrates that our model exhibits fairness across demographic and clinical subgroups, with no systematic bias favoring or disfavoring particular patient populations. Performance remains strong and consistent regardless of sex, diabetes status, age, or hypertension status.

Ordinal performance metrics

Standard multiclass metrics (accuracy, F1) treat all misclassifications equally, failing to account for the ordered nature of CKD stages. For example, predicting Stage 3 when the true label is Stage 4 (one-stage error) is clinically less severe than predicting Stage 1 (three-stage error). Ordinal-Aware Performance Metrics. 95% CIs from stratified bootstrap (1000 iterations).

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{a}$$\end{document} Quadratic weighted Cohen’s kappa: Agreement accounting for ordinal distance. Range: [-1, 1], where 1 = perfect agreement, 0 = chance, and quadratic weighting penalizes larger prediction errors more heavily. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{b}$$\end{document}MAE (stages): Mean absolute error in stage units. Lower is better. 0 = perfect, 1 = average 1-stage error, etc. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{c}$$\end{document}Adjacent accuracy: % of predictions within ±1 stage of true label. High adjacent accuracy means large errors (e.g., predicting Stage 1 when true is Stage 5) are rare. Our final student achieves QW-\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa$$\end{document} = 0.882, indicating strong ordinal agreement, with 98.2% of predictions within ±1 stage

Quadratic weighted Cohen’s kappa (QW-\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa$$\end{document}). Cohen’s kappa measures inter-rater agreement while accounting for chance. The quadratic weighting scheme penalizes larger disagreements quadratically: a 1-stage error incurs weight 1, a 2-stage error weight 4, a 3-stage error weight 9, etc. This aligns with clinical severity like confusing Stage 4 with Stage 5 (one level apart) is much less problematic than confusing Stage 1 with Stage 6 (five levels apart).

Our final student achieves QW-\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa$$\end{document} = 0.882 [0.847, 0.912], indicating strong ordinal agreement. For comparison, the teacher achieves 0.921 [0.889, 0.947], meaning the student retains 95.8% of teacher ordinal agreement. The baseline student (no tuning) shows poor ordinal performance (QW-\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa$$\end{document} = 0.189), confirming that our training enhancements (class balancing, ordinal loss, eGFR regression) are critical for preserving ordinal structure.

Mean absolute error (MAE) in stage units. MAE measures average prediction error in interpretable stage units. Our final student achieves MAE = 0.122 [0.089, 0.164] stages, meaning the average prediction error is approximately one-eighth of a stage. This is clinically negligible—the model’s predictions are nearly exact.

The teacher achieves MAE = 0.089 [0.062, 0.124], so the student’s ordinal error is 37% higher but still excellent in absolute terms. For reference, the baseline student has MAE = 1.832 stages (predicting nearly 2 stages off on average), and standard logistic regression achieves MAE = 0.489 stages. Our ordinal-aware training reduces MAE by 93% compared to baseline (1.832 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow$$\end{document} 0.122).

Adjacent-stage accuracy. This metric measures the percentage of predictions within ±1 stage of the true label. High adjacent accuracy indicates that large misclassifications (off by 2+ stages) are rare, which is critical for patient safety.

Our final student achieves 98.2% [96.4, 99.6] adjacent accuracy, meaning only 4 out of 225 test samples (1.8%) are misclassified by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ge$$\end{document}2 stages. The teacher achieves 98.7% [96.4, 100.0], indicating near-identical tolerance for large errors. In contrast, the baseline student achieves only 72.4% adjacent accuracy, meaning 62 samples (27.6%) are off by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ge$$\end{document}2 stages—a clinically unacceptable error rate.

Clinical significance. These ordinal metrics confirm that our model’s errors are clinically mild. When the model is wrong, it is typically wrong by only 1 stage (adjacent error), not 2+ stages (large error). For example, the model might predict Stage 3b when the true label is Stage 4, which is a borderline case clinically. Gross misclassifications (e.g., predicting Stage 1 for a Stage 5 patient) occur in <2% of cases.

This ordinal performance is a direct result of our training design: (i) CORN’s ordinal loss explicitly penalizes distant predictions more heavily, (ii) eGFR regression auxiliary task provides continuous supervision aligned with stage ordering, and (iii) monotonicity constraints enforce that higher creatinine \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow$$\end{document} higher predicted stage. Together, these components ensure the model respects the ordered nature of CKD progression.

Ablation and hyperparameter study

We ran 500 Optuna trials with a TPE sampler and a MedianPruner (three warm-up evaluations), exactly within the search space and inner-loop training protocol specified in Methods: Each trial trained up to 500 epochs (AdamW, weight decay \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-4}$$\end{document}, gradient clip 3.0) and the monotonicity ramp \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{\textrm{crea}}(t)=\alpha _{\textrm{final}}\cdot 2.4\cdot \min (1,t/400)$$\end{document}. The objective minimised \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1-\textrm{F}_1^{\textrm{macro}}$$\end{document} on the fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$15\%$$\end{document} calibration subset.

The linear ramp schedule \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{\text {crea}}(t) = \alpha _{\text {final}} \cdot \gamma \cdot \min (1, t/t_{\text {ramp}})$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_{\text {ramp}} = 400$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma = 2.4$$\end{document} was designed to decouple representation learning from constraint satisfaction. During early training (epochs 0–400), the network optimizes primarily for discrimination (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_{\text {CORN}}$$\end{document}) and knowledge transfer (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_{\text {KD}}$$\end{document}), allowing it to discover useful feature combinations without the monotonicity penalty restricting the hypothesis space. As the ramp progresses, the gradually increasing penalty steers the learned representations toward physiologically consistent mappings without disrupting already-formed decision boundaries. The multiplicative factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma = 2.4$$\end{document} ensures the effective penalty peaks at 0.24 (for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{\text {final}} = 0.10$$\end{document}), providing sufficient gradient magnitude to enforce monotonicity even as learning rates decay in later epochs. We observed stable convergence under this schedule i.e the composite loss \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}$$\end{document} decreased monotonically after an initial transient period, with no oscillations or divergence. For alternative schedules; constant penalty from epoch 0 or activation only in final epochs were explored during hyperparameter search but yielded inferior validation Macro-F1, supporting the chosen configuration.

The optimisation history (Fig. 8) shows rapid early gains within \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\approx 150$$\end{document}-250 trials, followed by diminishing returns; the best validation Macro-F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document} plateaus near 0.90 after trial \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\approx 420$$\end{document}. Hyperparameter importance from TPE fANOVA (Fig. 9) prioritises the composite-loss weights over capacity: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{\textrm{KD}}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{\textrm{final}}$$\end{document} together explain most of the variance, while H and D are secondary and p/\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document} are comparatively minor. Sensitivity to loss weights. We isolated the three loss weights while fixing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{\textrm{CE}}{=}0.6$$\end{document} and the ramp schedule. Table 18 reports representative settings; two-dimensional slices are visualised in Fig. 10 (Macro-F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document}) and Fig. 11 (ECE). Three patterns were consistent: (i) excessive distillation (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{\textrm{KD}}\!\gtrsim \!0.6$$\end{document}) harms both discrimination and calibration; (ii) a moderate eGFR weight (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \approx 0.10$$\end{document}-0.15) improves Macro-F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document} and ECE; (iii) increasing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{\textrm{final}}$$\end{document} tightens creatinine-monotonicity (violations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$<0.5\%$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{\textrm{final}}\!\ge \!0.08$$\end{document}) with small accuracy trade-offs when overly large. The selected setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\lambda _{\textrm{KD}}{=}0.30,\ \beta {=}0.12,\ \alpha _{\textrm{final}}{=}0.10\}$$\end{document} balances these effects and is used in the final student (Macro-F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1{=}0.904$$\end{document}, Macro-AUC 0.991, ECE \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3.5\%$$\end{document}, monotonicity violations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.4\%$$\end{document}).Table 18Sensitivity of the student to composite-loss weights (test split). Figures 10 and 11 show that the accuracy and calibration optima coincide near \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{\textrm{KD}}{\approx }0.3$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{\textrm{final}}{\in }[0.10,0.12]$$\end{document} for both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta {=}0.08$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta {=}0.12$$\end{document}, i.e. there is no accuracy-calibration trade-off in the region we ultimately select.

Repeating the search with 100, 250, and 500 trials yielded median best Macro-F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document} of 0.887, 0.898, and 0.904 respectively. Across five runs with fixed seeds, the final tuned configuration varied by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pm 0.004$$\end{document} Macro-F\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$_1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pm 0.2$$\end{document} pp ECE, indicating low variance under the safeguards described in Methods. The complete protocol required \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\approx 50$$\end{document} minutes on a single NVIDIA GeForce RTX 5090 GPU.

creatinine-monotonicity audit. Percentage of violations where the predicted expected stage decreases after increasing Scr by the indicated percentage. Lower is better.

Discussion

This work demonstrates that combining ordinal classification, probability calibration, and a physiology-informed monotonicity prior within a knowledge-distillation pipeline can yield multi-stage CKD predictions that are not only accurate but also more aligned with clinical reasoning. Encoding KDIGO stage ordering through an ordinal head reduces large-step misclassifications, which are clinically consequential, while probability-space distillation from a temperature-calibrated teacher transfers nuanced, well-calibrated class structure to a compact student5–7,24. The creatinine-monotonicity penalty further constrains the hypothesis space toward physiologically plausible mappings, a property that is often underemphasized in medical AI despite its direct relevance to clinical trust.

A central question is why this combination improves over classical deep learning for tabular data. Mechanistically, ordinal modelling reshapes the loss landscape to favour rank-consistent decision boundaries; empirically, this concentrates residual errors between adjacent stages rather than across distant ones. Calibration contributes a different axis of quality: well-calibrated probabilities are indispensable when predictions gate referrals or medication adjustments, because overconfidence can be harmful even when discrimination is high7,24. Knowledge distillation from a calibrated CatBoost teacher provides richer supervision than hard labels, especially in small-to-medium tabular datasets where soft class structure carries signal that plain one-hot targets discard. Finally, constraining the local gradient of the expected stage with respect to serum creatinine discourages counterintuitive mappings (higher Scr \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow$$\end{document} milder stage) that clinicians would rightly distrust.

Comparison with prior CKD prediction studies

To contextualize our contributions, we systematically compare our framework against the evolving landscape of CKD prediction research, synthesizing findings from recent systematic reviews23,47,48 covering 86+ studies (2019 to 2025) and examining advances across three dimensions: task formulation, methodological sophistication, and evaluation rigor.

Task formulation

The vast majority of CKD prediction studies continue to frame the problem as binary classification (CKD vs. no CKD), discarding the clinically meaningful ordinal structure of KDIGO staging. The systematic review by Iliyas et al. 23 found that deep learning approaches dominated recent publications (24% of 86 studies), yet primarily addressed binary detection rather than multi-stage classification. Islam et al. 15 achieved 98.33% accuracy with XGBoost on the UCI dataset; Halder et al. 49 reported 99.80% with their ML-CKDP framework; and Alturki et al. 50 achieved 98.97% with their TrioNet ensemble; but all addressed only binary CKD/non-CKD discrimination. While such high accuracies appear impressive, binary formulations conflate Stage 1 with Stage 5, offering no guidance on disease severity, treatment urgency, or progression monitoring. Among the minority of studies addressing multi-class staging, most treat stages as nominal categories: CKD.Net 51 proposed a hybrid CNN-LSTM model for multi-stage prediction but employed standard cross-entropy loss, penalizing Stage 3a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow$$\end{document}3b errors identically to Stage 1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow$$\end{document}5 errors. The recent explainable early-stage CKD prediction model by He et al. 52 used XGBoost with SHAP interpretability but focused only on early-stage (Stages 1-2) versus non-CKD classification. Our ordinal formulation explicitly encodes stage ordering through the CORN framework 5, achieving quadratic-weighted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa = 0.882$$\end{document} and concentrating 98.2% of predictions within \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pm 1$$\end{document} stage of the true label; metrics that prior nominal approaches neither optimized nor reported.

Methodological sophistication

Prior CKD studies have largely treated machine learning as black-box optimization, applying off-the-shelf classifiers without incorporating domain knowledge or uncertainty quantification. The comprehensive review by Gogoi and Valan 47 explicitly identified three critical gaps: (i) “calibration is rarely assessed,” (ii) “ordinal methods remain underexplored,” and (iii) “physics-informed constraints are absent.” Our framework directly addresses all three gaps through a unified architecture.

Ordinal classification. While ordinal regression has been successfully applied to medical imaging : Bérchez-Moreno et al. 53 demonstrated soft labelling with ordinal classification for knee osteoarthritis grading, and Liu et al. 54 proposed OR-DGN with ordinal regularization for disease grading and its application to tabular CKD data remains virtually unexplored. The recent deep learning ordinal classifier by Nxumalo et al. 55 applied threshold-based modeling to TabNet but did not evaluate on medical datasets. To our knowledge, no prior CKD staging framework employs cumulative link models like CORN 5 that explicitly encode rank-consistency through conditional probability chains.

Probability calibration. The clinical importance of calibrated predictions has been increasingly recognized 56,57, yet remains systematically neglected in CKD prediction. Huang et al. 56 demonstrated that ECE like factors, reliability diagrams, and calibration slopes are essential for clinical prediction models, while Lindenmeyer et al. 58 showed that “common deep learning approaches have a tendency towards overconfidence when faced with unfamiliar conditions”; a critical concern for CKD staging where borderline cases require honest uncertainty communication. The recent heart disease calibration study 59 demonstrated that isotonic calibration consistently improved probability reliability, yet no equivalent calibration analysis exists for CKD multi-stage prediction. Our framework integrates temperature-scaled knowledge distillation 7,12 specifically to transfer calibration properties from an ensemble teacher, achieving ECE of 3.5% across six CKD stages.

Physics-informed constraints. The integration of domain knowledge into neural networks has emerged as a transformative paradigm across biomedical applications 60,61. Ahmadi et al. 60 reviewed physics-informed machine learning for medical imaging, demonstrating how physical constraints enhance interpretability and reduce data requirements. For physiological signal modeling, PINNs have been applied to cardiovascular flows 62 and cardiac electrodynamics 63. However, the application of monotonicity constraints (a fundamental physiological prior) to tabular clinical prediction remains novel. Al-Salman and Cevik 19 proposed structure-function consistency for kidney imaging, but their U-Net architecture targeted radiological data rather than tabular biomarkers. Our creatinine-monotonicity constraint (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \hat{y}/\partial x_{\text {Scr}} \ge 0$$\end{document}) encodes the physiological invariant that higher serum creatinine indicates worse kidney function, reducing violation rates to 0.4% while maintaining discriminative performance; which is a methodological contribution with no direct precedent in CKD prediction.

Knowledge distillation for healthcare. Knowledge distillation has gained traction in medical AI for model compression 64 and federated learning 65. MedAlmighty 66 demonstrated large vision model distillation for medical image classification, while Yang et al. 67 applied distillation for medical segmentation. However, these applications focus on image data; distillation for tabular clinical prediction with explicit calibration transfer remains underexplored. Our teacher-student framework uniquely explores ensemble diversity (RF, XGBoost, LightGBM) with temperature scaling to simultaneously transfer discriminative knowledge and calibration properties to the ordinal student network.

Evaluation rigor

Many prior CKD studies report only accuracy on small, single-center datasets without confidence intervals, calibration metrics, or ordinal-aware evaluation. Studies using the 400-patient UCI dataset 14 are particularly susceptible to overfitting, with several reporting near-perfect accuracy (>99%) that likely reflects data leakage or non-independent test sets 49. The recent multicenter CKD prediction study by He et al. 52 represents an improvement with 21,000+ patients and external validation, yet still omits calibration assessment entirely. Chen et al. 68 developed ensemble models for CKD progression prediction with external validation across five centers, achieving C-index of 0.89 for renal replacement therapy prediction but focused on binary progression outcomes rather than multi-stage classification and did not report calibration metrics.

In contrast, we report: (i) six discrimination metrics (accuracy, balanced accuracy, Macro-F1, weighted F1, Cohen’s \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa$$\end{document}, Matthews correlation coefficient) with 95% bootstrap confidence intervals; (ii) three calibration metrics (ECE, MCE, Brier score) under multiple binning strategies (10, 15, 20 bins) with reliability diagrams; (iii) three ordinal-aware metrics (quadratic-weighted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa$$\end{document}, mean absolute error, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pm 1$$\end{document}-stage accuracy); (iv) explicit monotonicity audits under creatinine perturbation; and (v) subgroup analyses across sex, age, diabetes, and hypertension status demonstrating consistent performance (Table 15). This comprehensive evaluation template exceeds the reporting standards of all prior CKD prediction studies we reviewed and aligns with emerging guidelines for trustworthy AI in healthcare 69.

Methodological novelty

Table 21 summarizes the comparison with representative prior studies. To our knowledge, our framework is the first to integrate: (i) ordinal classification via CORN for CKD staging, (ii) calibrated knowledge distillation from ensemble teachers, and (iii) physiologically-informed monotonicity constraints within a unified architecture. While each component has precedent in isolation, their synergistic combination (and application to multi-stage CKD prediction) represents a methodological advance that addresses the three critical gaps identified by recent systematic reviews 23,47.

Limitations of comparison

Direct quantitative comparison with prior studies is confounded by heterogeneous datasets (UCI, hospital registries, claims data), label definitions (binary, multi-class, ordinal), preprocessing pipelines, and evaluation protocols. Our cohort from Al-Ramadi Teaching Hospital differs demographically from Western populations used in most prior studies, and our MDRD-derived labels introduce circularity that inflates apparent performance. We therefore emphasize methodological contributions (the integration of ordinal learning, calibration, and monotonicity constraints) over claims of state-of-the-art performance, recognizing that the framework’s value lies in its transferability to study designs that eliminate these confounds rather than in benchmark comparisons on heterogeneous datasets. The systematic reviews by Iliyas et al. 23 and Gogoi and Valan 47 both call for “additional development methods to enhance accuracy, efficiency, and the use of explainable AI techniques”; our framework directly responds to this call by providing a principled architecture that unifies ordinal structure, calibration, and physiological plausibility.Table 21Comparison with representative prior CKD prediction studies.
