Limitations

Several critical limitations temper these findings and must be acknowledged for appropriate interpretation of results.

Target leakage and circular reasoning

The most significant limitation of this study is target leakage: serum creatinine (Scr) is used both to derive CKD stage labels via the MDRD equation (Eq. 2) and as an input predictor. Our comprehensive five-experiment analysis (Table 14) quantifies this circularity. When serum creatinine was removed from the predictor set while retaining MDRD-derived labels, performance degraded severely: the teacher’s Macro-F1 dropped from 0.935 to 0.514 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-45.0\%$$\end{document}), and the student’s Macro-F1 fell from 0.904 to 0.524 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-42.0\%$$\end{document}). This 40–45% performance drop confirms that prediction is predominantly driven by the circularity between Scr as a predictor and Scr-derived labels, rather than the discovery of independent biomarker patterns for CKD staging.

Moreover, restricting the model to only the three MDRD equation components (Age, Sex, Scr) yielded the highest performance across all experiments: teacher Macro-F1 = 0.945 and Macro-AUC = 0.997, exceeding the full 13-feature configuration by 1.1% in F1 and 0.2% in AUC (Table 13). This finding has profound implications: the additional ten clinical features (WBC, RBC, HGB, HCT, PLT, Urea, UA, Glucose, Hypertension, Diabetes) provide minimal incremental value beyond what is already captured by the eGFR equation inputs. In essence, our full-feature model is learning a complex, non-linear refinement of the MDRD formula rather than uncovering novel diagnostic pathways. This finding contradicts the implicit assumption that machine learning can extract independent signal from routine laboratory panels to improve upon existing clinical formulae.

This circularity is not unique to our work; it is a pervasive challenge in studies predicting CKD stage from routine biomarkers when measured eGFR (the gold standard) is unavailable. The circularity is inherent to eGFR-based staging, independent of the specific estimation equation used (MDRD vs. CKD-EPI)3,4. However, the methodological contributions of this study (integrating ordinal learning, calibration, and monotonicity constraints) remain valid and transferable to study designs that eliminate target leakage.

Incomplete KDIGO staging

As we have already stated; complete KDIGO 2012 staging requires both eGFR categories (G1–G5) and albuminuria categories (A1–A3). Our dataset does not include albuminuria measurements (urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio, ACR) due to limited routine testing at Al-Ramadi Teaching Hospital. Consequently, this study performs eGFR-grade classification along the eGFR axis only, not full KDIGO staging. This represents an incomplete operationalization of current clinical guidelines.

While eGFR dominates immediate clinical decisions like medication dosing, nephrology referral, dialysis planning; albuminuria primarily refines long-term risk stratification for progression and cardiovascular events. The absence of ACR limits our model’s applicability to comprehensive risk assessment and may underestimate progression risk in patients with high albuminuria despite preserved eGFR. For example, Stage G2A3 patients (eGFR 60–89 with severely increased albuminuria) are at higher cardiovascular and progression risk than Stage G3aA1 patients (eGFR 45–59 with normal albuminuria), yet our model cannot distinguish these scenarios72. Future datasets incorporating albuminuria would enable full KDIGO staging and more nuanced risk stratification.

Single-center cohort and generalizability concerns

The cohort is derived from a single center (Al-Ramadi Teaching Hospital, Iraq) and is ethnically homogeneous (Iraqi population). This raises three generalizability concerns. First, geographic and ethnic specificity: GFR estimation equations show variable accuracy across populations28. Our choice of MDRD over CKD-EPI was based on reported better performance in Middle-Eastern cohorts at eGFR < 60 mL/min/1.73 m\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^2$$\end{document}, but this calibration may not hold in other populations. Second, spectrum effects: Hospital-based sampling introduces selection bias toward more severe disease (33.1% Stage 5 patients in our cohort vs. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sim$$\end{document}1% in general population). The model may underperform in primary care settings with milder, early-stage CKD where the clinical challenge is distinguishing Stage 1 from Stage 2, not identifying advanced kidney failure. Third, healthcare system context: Practice patterns, laboratory standardization, and patient demographics vary substantially across healthcare systems. External validation across diverse populations (multi-ethnic cohorts, multi-center studies, different care settings) is mandatory before clinical deployment.

Statistical and methodological constraints

We employed a single 70/30 stratified split rather than nested cross-validation. While we report stratified bootstrap confidence intervals (1000 iterations) that quantify sampling variability within the test set, these CIs do not capture variability across different data partitions. Nested cross-validation (outer loop for performance estimation, inner loop for hyperparameter tuning) would provide more robust uncertainty quantification but was computationally prohibitive, estimated to require >1 week on RTX 5090 GPU for 500 Optuna trials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times$$\end{document} 500 epochs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times$$\end{document} 5 folds. We adopted the pragmatic approach recommended for single-split evaluation in resource-constrained settings29: stratified bootstrap CIs on the held-out test set, combined with explicit acknowledgment of this limitation. Our results should be interpreted as performance estimates on this particular train-test partition, with CIs quantifying sampling variability conditional on the split.

Expected calibration error (ECE) depends on binning strategy, and fixed equal-width bins can under- or over-estimate miscalibration with small sample sizes (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=225$$\end{document} test samples)36. We addressed this by reporting three binning strategies: fixed-width, equal-frequency, equal-mass; and adopting equal-frequency ECE as our primary metric (2.8% [1.9, 3.8] vs. 3.5% [2.4, 4.7] for fixed-width). However, class-wise ECE for minority stages, especially Stage 4 with only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=32$$\end{document} test samples, has higher variance. The moderate miscalibration observed for Stage 4 (equal-frequency ECE = 4.3% [3.1, 5.8]) likely reflects this small sample size combined with the clinical heterogeneity of this transition region between moderate and severe CKD.

Standard classification metrics such as accuracy and F1-score treat all misclassifications equally, failing to account for the ordered nature of CKD stages. We supplemented these with ordinal-aware metrics ie. quadratic weighted Cohen’s \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa$$\end{document} = 0.882, mean absolute error = 0.122 stages, 98.2% adjacent accuracy; to align evaluation with clinical stage-distance costs. However, these should be interpreted alongside clinician input on acceptable error profiles, as the quadratic weighting scheme may not perfectly reflect clinical utility functions for different misclassification patterns.

We used predictive mean matching (PMM) for missing data imputation (Glucose 4.0%, Uric acid 2.7%, Serum creatinine 1.3%), which improves plausibility over mean or median substitution but understates uncertainty26,27. Multiple imputation with pooling via Rubin’s rules73 would yield more defensible inferences by propagating imputation uncertainty into parameter estimates and confidence intervals, but adds computational complexity and was not implemented in this study. The low missingness rates (<5% for all variables) somewhat mitigates this concern, as single imputation is generally adequate when missingness is minimal and missing-completely-at-random assumptions are plausible.

Monotonicity constraint limitations

The creatinine-monotonicity penalty \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_{\textrm{mono}}$$\end{document} enforces a local (gradient-based, pointwise) constraint as it penalises network configurations in which the first-order derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_{\textrm{mono}}$$\end{document} is negative at the specific training sample \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x}_i$$\end{document}. Formally, local monotonicity at a point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x}_0$$\end{document} means \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\partial \hat{s}}{\partial \textbf{x}_i}$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ge$$\end{document} 0. This is a condition on the instantaneous slope of the function at one point. It is computed via automatic differentiation, which returns exact gradients for the piecewise-linear operations in our residual MLP. The ramp schedule \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{\text {crea}}(t) = \alpha _{\text {final}} \cdot 2.4 \cdot \min (1, t/{\text {400}})$$\end{document} ensures that the penalty is increasingly enforced as training progresses, allowing coarse discrimination to be learned first.

Global monotonicity is the stronger requirement that, for all x in the input domain and all finite perturbation magnitudes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta$$\end{document} > 0; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{s}(x + \delta e_{Scr})$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ge$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{s}(x)$$\end{document}.

This is a function-class property, as it must hold everywhere in the input space, not just at sampled points, and for perturbations of any magnitude, not just infinitesimal ones. Local enforcement does not imply global monotonicity for three reasons. First, a ReLU network can have positive gradient at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x}_0$$\end{document} and negative gradient at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{s}(x_0 + \delta e_{Scr})$$\end{document}, because each ReLU unit has a discontinuous derivative at its kink; the function can locally increase and then decrease as Scr is raised. Second, the penalty is applied only at training data points; regions of input space not covered by training samples receive no direct gradient supervision. Third, the joint trajectory of multiple features; for example, increasing Scr and Urea simultaneously, as occurs naturally in CKD progression is not constrained by a univariate penalty on Scr alone.

Table 20 provides the key practical evidence. By perturbing Scr upward by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\in$$\end{document} {5%, 10%, 20%} on each of the 225 held-out test samples (all other features held fixed) and counting the fraction of cases in which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{s}(x)$$\end{document} decreases, we assess practical, finite-step monotonicity at unseen input points. The final tuned student achieves violation rates of 0.6% [0.0, 1.6], 0.9% [0.2, 2.0], and 1.4% [0.4, 2.7] respectively; which is substantially below the unconstrained teacher (1.8%/3.1%/5.4%) and the baseline student (4.1%/6.8%/10.5%). These figures demonstrate that local enforcement generalises effectively to finite perturbations within the clinically relevant range, providing a strong empirical approximation to the global guarantee even without architectural constraints. Nevertheless, the audit covers only the 225 test samples and three perturbation levels; it cannot rule out violations at unobserved input combinations or for very large creatinine elevations beyond the dataset range. Architectures that enforce global monotonicity by construction such as low-dimensional lattice networks applied to the constrained features, or min-max sub-networks remain the rigorous alternative, and are potential future direction.

The monotonicity prior, as implemented, is a local (differential) constraint (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \hat{s}/\partial \text {Scr} \ge 0$$\end{document}) and does not guarantee global monotonicity under large finite perturbations or feature interactions. Our finite-difference audits (Table 20) provide empirical evidence of good local behavior; as only 0.4% violations at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+5\%$$\end{document} Scr perturbation, 0.9% at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+10\%$$\end{document} Scr, and 1.4% at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+20\%$$\end{document} Scr. However, stronger guarantees could be explored with architectures that enforce partial monotonicity by construction, such as lattice networks or min-max architectures that constrain the function class to be monotone by design rather than via gradient penalties.

At this point, we should also discuss why full lattice modelling is infeasible for this particular dataset. A lattice network represents a d-dimensional function over a grid of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^d$$\end{document} vertices. For our 13-feature input space, the vertex count is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{13}$$\end{document} = 8,192 (for L = 2 keypoints per dimension) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{13}$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\approx$$\end{document} 67 million (for L = 4, the standard TensorFlow Lattice default). Our training set contains only 525 samples. Even at the minimum resolution of L = 2, the lattice has 15.6 times more parameters than training samples, making estimation statistically intractable and virtually guaranteeing memorisation of the training set. At L = 4, the vertex count exceeds the training set size by a factor of approximately 127,000 and surpasses the memory capacity of a standard GPU. Full 13-dimensional lattice modelling is therefore computationally and statistically infeasible for our dataset. Furthermore, of our 13 features, 3 are binary nominal (Gender, Hypertension, Diabetes mellitus) with no natural monotone ordering, and 7 continuous features (WBC, RBC, HGB, HCT, PLT, Uric acid, Glucose) do not have an established directional relationship with CKD stage that would justify a monotone constraint; enforcing global monotonicity over these dimensions would be clinically unwarranted and unnecessarily restrictive. A selective low-dimensional lattice applied exclusively to the constrained features reduces the vertex count to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$4^3$$\end{document} = 64 (d = 3, L = 4) and fully tractable relative to our 525 training samples. This selective hybrid approach, combining a lattice sub-network for constrained variables with a standard residual MLP for the remainder, is identified as the principal future direction for strengthening monotonicity guarantees (see Future Directions).

Moreover, we only enforced monotonicity with respect to serum creatinine. The decision to constrain only creatinine is not arbitrary but reflects a tiered assessment of the strength and individual-level reliability of each feature’s monotone relationship with CKD stage. Scr is physiologically exact (Scr \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\propto$$\end{document} 1/GFR by definition), individually reliable (directional robustness is independent of confounders such as muscle mass), codified in the KDIGO staging criteria, and encoded in the MDRD and CKD-EPI equations the model is trained alongside. Urea and Age both show population level monotone trends, but are confounded at the individual level (protein intake and GI bleeding for Urea; preserved eGFR in elderly patients for Age). For the remaining nine features (WBC, RBC, HGB, HCT, PLT, Uric acid, Glucose, Gender, Hypertension, Diabetes mellitus); none has an established individual-level directional relationship with CKD stage warranting a monotone constraint, and enforcing one would introduce clinically unwarranted rigidity.

Temporal and longitudinal limitations

CKD is defined by a persistence of at least 3 months of reduced eGFR or markers of kidney damage2; not single encounters. Our dataset represents cross-sectional snapshots without longitudinal follow-up to confirm persistence of abnormalities. Single low eGFR measurements can occur due to acute kidney injury (AKI), dehydration, or laboratory error, leading to false-positive CKD diagnoses if persistence is not verified. If repeated measures exist in the source data, patient-level clustering and temporal leakage must be ruled out to preserve independence of observations assumed in our statistical analyses.

The absence of temporal data also prevents us from addressing the most clinically relevant question: predicting CKD progression. Forecasting trajectory changes ;for example, predicting which Stage 2 patients will progress to Stage 3 within 2 years based on baseline biomarkers would eliminate the circular reasoning inherent in cross-sectional staging. Progression risk matters more than current stage for treatment planning: a Stage 3a patient with stable function for 10 years requires less aggressive management than a Stage 2 patient with rapidly declining eGFR. Longitudinal modeling frameworks such as joint models for longitudinal and survival data, or recurrent neural networks for trajectory prediction, represent essential future directions but require cohorts with repeated measurements over time.

Clinical implications

Despite the limitations outlined above, this work offers several insights relevant to clinical practice and medical AI development.

Methodological soundness despite target leakage

While the target leakage issue substantially limits the clinical interpretability of our stage predictions, the methodological framework combining ordinal learning, probability calibration, and physiological constraints via knowledge distillation remains sound and generalizable. The student model achieved 96.7% retention of teacher discriminative performance (Macro-F1: 0.904 vs. 0.935) with 97.8% calibration retention and 97.9% resolution retention (Table 8)). This demonstrates that the knowledge distillation pipeline successfully transfers both discrimination and calibration from complex ensemble to neural network, even in the presence of leakage.

The framework can be directly applied to study designs that eliminate circular reasoning. First, progression forecasting: predicting trajectory changes such as Stage 2 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow$$\end{document} Stage 3 within 2 years from baseline biomarkers, avoiding reliance on contemporaneous eGFR for both input and output. Second, measured GFR studies: using gold-standard iohexol clearance, inulin clearance, or chromium-51 EDTA clearance instead of estimated eGFR derived from creatinine equations. Third, hard outcome prediction: predicting end-stage renal disease (ESRD), dialysis initiation, or all-cause mortality from features independent of Age/Sex/Scr, sacrificing immediate staging capability but enabling genuine biomarker discovery for progression risk.

Importance of ordinal structure in disease staging

Our ordinal-aware approach concentrated errors between adjacent stages i.e. 98.2% adjacent accuracy rather than across distant stages, with mean absolute error of only 0.122 stages (Table 16). This is clinically meaningful: confusing Stage 3b with Stage 4 (one level apart, eGFR difference of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sim$$\end{document}10 mL/min/1.73 m\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^2$$\end{document}) has minor clinical consequences compared to confusing Stage 1 with Stage 5 (five levels apart, eGFR difference of >75 mL/min/1.73 m\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^2$$\end{document}). The former might result in slightly premature nephrology referral or conservative medication dosing; the latter could lead to catastrophic errors such as nephrotoxic drug administration to a patient with kidney failure or delayed dialysis initiation for a patient misclassified as having normal kidney function.

The baseline student without ordinal structure achieved only 72.4% adjacent accuracy, meaning 27.6% of errors spanned \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ge$$\end{document}2 stages a clinically unacceptable error profile that would undermine trust if deployed. The CORN ordinal head5 embeds stage ordering directly into the network architecture through cumulative logistic mappings, making the model structure-aware of disease progression. This approach is transferable to other ordinal medical classification tasks where misclassification costs scale with distance: cancer staging (TNM system), cardiovascular disease severity (NYHA functional class I–IV), Alzheimer’s disease staging (Clinical Dementia Rating 0–3), and diabetes progression (pre-diabetes, controlled, uncontrolled based on HbA1c categories).

Calibration quality and clinical decision support

Well-calibrated probability estimates are essential for clinical decision support7,24. Overconfident predictions can lead to inappropriate interventions for example, premature dialysis access creation for a patient with miscalibrated high-risk prediction or delayed referrals when the model underestimates risk. Our student achieved expected calibration error of 3.5% using fixed-width binning and 2.8% using equal-frequency binning, indicating that predicted probabilities closely match empirical frequencies across confidence levels.

Critically, class-wise calibration analysis (Table 7) revealed heterogeneous quality across disease severity. Stages 1–3 representing early-to-moderate CKD showed good calibration with equal-frequency ECE of 2.9–3.6%. Stage 4 exhibited moderate miscalibration (ECE = 4.3% [3.1, 5.8]), likely due to small sample size (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=32$$\end{document}) and the clinical complexity of this transition region between moderate and severe CKD where presentations overlap and progression rates vary widely. Stages 5–6 representing kidney failure achieved excellent calibration (ECE < 2.5%), which is clinically reassuring. These patients require immediate high-stakes decisions; timing of dialysis initiation, choice between hemodialysis versus peritoneal dialysis, vascular access creation, kidney transplant evaluation; and well-calibrated probability estimates directly support shared decision-making with patients about the risks and benefits of different renal replacement therapy options.

The Brier score decomposition (Table 8) further clarifies that our student’s modest Brier increase (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+0.011$$\end{document} versus teacher) reflects approximately equal contributions from both calibration loss (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta$$\end{document}Reliability = \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+0.004$$\end{document}) and discrimination loss (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta$$\end{document}Resolution = \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-0.015$$\end{document}). The student retains \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$>97\%$$\end{document} of the teacher’s performance on both calibration and discrimination dimensions, demonstrating successful knowledge distillation. This balance is important: some knowledge distillation methods sacrifice calibration to maximize discrimination, but medical decision support requires both properties.

Calibrated probabilities in nephrology practice

Beyond point predictions, calibrated probability estimates enable nuanced clinical reasoning that aligns with how nephrologists naturally approach diagnostic uncertainty. Consider a patient with borderline laboratory values where the model outputs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{p} = (0.05, 0.12, 0.38, 0.32, 0.09, 0.04)$$\end{document} across Stages 1–6. Rather than simply reporting the argmax prediction (Stage 3a), the full distribution conveys clinically actionable information: there is a 70% probability of moderate CKD (Stages 3a–3b combined), a 13% probability of severe disease (Stages 4–5), and meaningful uncertainty that warrants closer monitoring. An overconfident but miscalibrated model might instead output \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{p} = (0.01, 0.02, 0.91, 0.04, 0.01, 0.01)$$\end{document}, falsely suggesting diagnostic certainty and potentially discouraging appropriate follow-up testing.

Calibrated probabilities directly support several key nephrology workflows. For referral triage, primary care physicians can use probability thresholds to prioritize specialist consultations: patients with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P(\text {Stage} \ge 4) > 0.3$$\end{document} might warrant urgent nephrology referral, whereas those with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P(\text {Stage} \le 2) > 0.8$$\end{document} can be safely managed in primary care with periodic monitoring. For medication dosing, many drugs require renal adjustment at specific eGFR thresholds like, when a patient’s predicted stage distribution straddles such a threshold (e.g., 45% Stage 3a vs. 40% Stage 3b at the 45 mL/min boundary), clinicians can make informed decisions about conservative dosing versus standard dosing based on the probability mass below the threshold rather than relying on a single point estimate. For dialysis planning, the probability of Stage 5 informs the urgency of arteriovenous fistula creation, which requires 3-6 months maturation; a patient with 25% probability of Stage 5 within the confidence interval warrants earlier vascular surgery referral than one with 5% probability, even if both have the same point prediction.

Furthermore, calibrated probabilities facilitate shared decision-making with patients. Communicating “your kidney function test suggests moderate kidney disease, with about a 15% chance it could be more severe” is more honest and actionable than “you have Stage 3a CKD.” Patients can better understand why additional testing is recommended, why certain medications are being adjusted, and what their prognosis entails. This transparency aligns with patient-centered care principles and may improve treatment adherence by helping patients understand the rationale behind clinical recommendations.

Subgroup fairness and generalizability

Our subgroup analysis (Table 15) revealed no statistically significant performance differences across demographic and clinical subgroups. Comparing male versus female patients, Macro-F1 was 0.897 versus 0.912 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=0.78$$\end{document}, McNemar’s test). Diabetic versus non-diabetic patients showed Macro-F1 of 0.881 versus 0.917 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=0.24$$\end{document}). Younger versus older patients (<65 vs. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ge$$\end{document}65 years) achieved Macro-F1 of 0.889 versus 0.915 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=0.45$$\end{document}). Hypertensive versus non-hypertensive patients had Macro-F1 of 0.906 versus 0.895 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=0.62$$\end{document}). All pairwise comparisons yielded \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p > 0.05$$\end{document} with substantially overlapping confidence intervals, indicating no systematic bias.

This demonstrates that the model exhibits algorithmic fairness across demographic and clinical subgroups within our single-center Iraqi cohort. However, this fairness analysis is limited in two ways. First, it assesses only equal accuracy (same error rates across groups), not equal calibration or equal positive predictive value, which are distinct fairness criteria relevant in different clinical contexts. Second, fairness within a homogeneous population does not guarantee fairness across heterogeneous populations. The model may exhibit differential performance when applied to populations with different ethnic backgrounds, socioeconomic status, or healthcare access patterns. External validation in diverse populations is essential to assess whether fairness generalizes beyond our development cohort.

The consistent performance across diabetes and hypertension status is particularly noteworthy given that these are the two leading causes of CKD globally. Diabetic nephropathy and hypertensive nephrosclerosis have distinct pathophysiologic mechanisms and progression patterns, yet our model maintains similar discriminative and calibration performance in both subgroups. This suggests that the combination of ordinal structure, multi-task learning (via eGFR regression), and physiological constraints enables the model to generalize across different CKD etiologies rather than overfitting to a single disease pathway.

Computational efficiency for clinical deployment

The student model achieves sub-millisecond inference(0.8 ms on NVIDIA RTX 5090 GPU)and requires only 9.5 MB of memory in fp32 precision. This makes it suitable for multiple deployment scenarios. First, point-of-care deployment: Real-time risk stratification in primary care clinics or emergency departments where immediate triage decisions are needed and computational resources may be limited. Second, EHR integration: Low-latency predictions within clinical workflows without dedicated GPU infrastructure, enabling batch scoring of hospitalized patients during morning rounds or automated screening of laboratory results as they are reported. Third, mobile health applications: Edge deployment on resource-constrained devices for chronic disease monitoring in home settings or rural clinics without reliable internet connectivity.

The 150\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times$$\end{document} size reduction versus the teacher (2.37M vs. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sim$$\end{document}350M parameters) was achieved while retaining 96.7% of discriminative performance, demonstrating successful model compression for clinical deployment. For comparison, modern large language models require 10–100 GB of memory and >1 second inference per query, which would be prohibitive for real-time clinical decision support. Our compact student can be deployed on commodity hardware (laptop CPU, mobile device, or embedded system ) without requiring specialized accelerators.

The efficiency gains also have practical implications for healthcare equity. Smaller models are more deployable in resource-constrained settings likerural hospitals, developing countries, mobile clinics; where GPU infrastructure is unavailable or cost-prohibitive. If clinical utility is demonstrated in prospective studies, the compact architecture could enable broader dissemination of AI-assisted CKD screening and management beyond academic medical centers.

Monotonicity and clinical plausibility

The creatinine-monotonicity constraint (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \hat{s}/\partial \text {Scr} \ge 0$$\end{document}) enforces that, ceteris paribus, higher serum creatinine should not imply a milder stage. Our final tuned student achieved only 0.4% violations at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+5\%$$\end{document} Scr perturbation, compared to 4.1% for the baseline student and 1.8% for the unconstrained teacher (Table 20). This physiological consistency is critical for clinical trust: clinicians will rightfully reject models that produce biologically implausible predictions, such as predicting Stage 2 for a patient with Scr = 8.0 mg/dL (which corresponds to severe kidney failure requiring dialysis).

However, monotonicity alone cannot overcome the fundamental target leakage issue. The constraint ensures local physiological consistency; increasing creatinine within a small range (±5–20%) does not decrease predicted stage; but does not address the circular reasoning whereby the model is essentially learning to refine the MDRD equation that generated the training labels. This distinction is important: physiological plausibility is a necessary but not sufficient condition for clinical validity.

The relatively low violation rates compared to the teacher (0.4% vs. 1.8% at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+5\%$$\end{document} Scr) demonstrate that explicit architectural constraints outperform implicit learning of physical laws from data alone. Even a well-calibrated ensemble trained on thousands of examples occasionally produces violations, whereas our gradient-penalized neural network with ramped enforcement (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _{\text {final}}=0.10$$\end{document}, ramp schedule \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho (t)=2.4 \cdot \min (1, t/400)$$\end{document}) concentrates violations below 0.5% for moderate perturbations. This suggests that encoding domain knowledge via soft constraints during training, rather than hoping the model discovers physical laws from correlations, is a reliable path toward building trustworthy medical AI.

Future directions

To advance CKD prediction beyond the limitations of this study, we outline a roadmap across three timescales with specific priorities and feasibility considerations.

Pathways beyond circular reasoning

To eliminate target leakage and enable genuine biomarker discovery, future research must adopt fundamentally different study designs. The most feasible near-term approach is trajectory forecasting: predicting CKD progression such as Stage 2 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow$$\end{document} Stage 3 within 2 years from baseline biomarkers, avoiding reliance on contemporaneous eGFR for both input and output. This reframes the task as time-to-event or ordinal progression prediction, which eliminates circular reasoning while remaining clinically actionable; progression risk matters more than current stage for treatment planning. Longitudinal cohorts with at least two time points separated by 1–3 years are required, such as the Chronic Renal Insufficiency Cohort (CRIC), UK Biobank, or Framingham Heart Study. However, larger sample sizes are needed to observe sufficient progression events, and survival analysis frameworks must account for competing risks such as death or kidney transplant before progression occurs. We prioritize this as the highest-priority direction due to feasibility within 1–2 years and direct clinical relevance.

Medium-term approaches include measured GFR studies: using gold-standard iohexol clearance, inulin clearance, or chromium-51 EDTA clearance instead of estimated eGFR derived from creatinine equations. This eliminates equation-based circularity and provides ground truth for GFR, enabling validation of novel biomarker panels. However, measured GFR is expensive ($500–1000 per test), invasive (requires timed blood/urine collection over 4–6 hours), and rarely performed in routine practice. Specialized research cohorts such as the European Kidney Function Consortium or CKD-EPI validation cohorts typically have modest sample sizes (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=500$$\end{document}–2000), which limits statistical power for machine learning studies. Generalizability to real-world practice is uncertain given the selected populations enrolled in measured GFR studies. We assign this medium priority for 3–5 year timescale, valuable for biomarker validation but limited scalability.

Alternatively, hard outcome prediction predicts clinically meaningful endpoints; ESRD, dialysis initiation, doubling of serum creatinine, cardiovascular events, all-cause mortality; from features entirely independent of Age/Sex/Scr. This sacrifices immediate staging capability but focuses on what ultimately matters to patients and clinicians. Electronic health record (EHR) databases with longitudinal follow-up and outcome adjudication are required, such as Kaiser Permanente KPNC, UK Clinical Practice Research Datalink, or Medicare/Medicaid claims databases. Challenges include long follow-up required (5–10 years for sufficient ESRD events), incomplete outcome ascertainment in claims databases, and survival bias whereby sicker patients are censored by death before reaching ESRD. We assign this medium priority for 3–5 year timescale, with high clinical value but substantial infrastructure requirements.

Long-term exploratory approaches include multi-modal biomarker discovery: integrating cystatin C, proteomics panels, metabolomics, imaging biomarkers (renal ultrasound, MRI), and genetics to discover novel disease mechanisms independent of creatinine-based eGFR. Large multi-omics cohorts with comprehensive phenotyping are required, such as UK Biobank (500,000 participants with genomics, proteomics, metabolomics) or All of Us Research Program (1 million participants target). However, proteomics panels cost $500–5000 per sample, high dimensionality requires very large sample sizes (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n>10,000$$\end{document}) to avoid overfitting, interpretability is challenging for high-dimensional omics data, and regulatory pathways for novel biomarkers are uncertain. We assign this lower priority for 5+ year timescale, primarily exploratory research value with limited near-term clinical translation.

In summary, we recommend prioritizing trajectory forecasting (1–2 years) as the most feasible path forward, followed by pooled measured GFR studies (3–5 years) for biomarker validation when resources permit. Hard outcome prediction should be pursued in parallel where large EHR databases with outcome data are available. Multi-modal approaches remain exploratory and should be reserved for well-funded, long-term research programs with dedicated biomarker discovery aims.

External validation and multi-center studies

Our single-center Iraqi cohort limits generalizability, necessitating a phased validation strategy. Phase 1 involves internal-external validation over 6–12 months to assess temporal stability and local generalizability. Prospective validation on a new patient cohort from Al-Ramadi Teaching Hospital collected during 2025–2026 would track discrimination (Macro-F1, Macro-AUC), calibration (ECE, Brier decomposition), and ordinal performance (quadratic weighted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa$$\end{document}, mean absolute error) over time. We expect modest calibration drift due to changing patient mix; for example, if diabetes prevalence increases or laboratory assays change; requiring recalibration via temperature scaling. This phase establishes baseline model behavior and identifies the need for periodic recalibration protocols.

Phase 2 involves multi-center validation over 1–2 years to assess geographic and ethnic generalizability. External validation in 3–5 centers across different regions; Middle East, Europe, North America, Asia; with at least 500 patients per site ensures stable performance estimates. Analysis should compare performance across sites, identify systematic biases (for example, if the model systematically under-predicts severity in Asian populations due to lower muscle mass and correspondingly lower creatinine), and assess whether site-specific recalibration or complete model retraining is needed. We expect performance degradation in ethnically diverse populations due to GFR equation miscalibration, potentially requiring region-specific models or adoption of race/ethnicity-adjusted eGFR equations such as CKD-EPI 20213.

Phase 3 involves a randomized controlled trial (RCT) over 3–5 years to assess clinical utility: does model-guided care improve patient outcomes? A cluster-randomized trial comparing model-assisted versus standard care for CKD management; for example, decision support for nephrology referral timing or medication dosing adjustments; with primary composite outcome of CKD progression, cardiovascular events, hospitalization, or death would provide definitive evidence. Secondary outcomes include healthcare utilization (specialist referrals, emergency visits, hospitalizations), patient-reported outcomes (quality of life, treatment satisfaction), and clinician satisfaction with the decision support tool. Approximately 50 primary care clinics with 5000 patients (2500 per arm) would provide 80% power to detect 15% relative risk reduction in the composite outcome. We expect modest improvement in guideline-concordant care processes; more appropriate referrals, more timely interventions; but uncertain impact on hard outcomes without an integrated intervention component that specifies actions based on model predictions.

Enhancing calibration and uncertainty quantification

Our adoption of equal-frequency ECE represents a step toward robust calibration assessment36, but further refinements are possible. Adaptive quantile binning with varying bin widths by sample density could provide more stable estimates in regions of the confidence space with few samples. Class-wise temperature scaling; fitting separate temperature parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_k^*$$\end{document} for each CKD stage k; could address the heterogeneous miscalibration observed across stages, particularly the elevated ECE for Stage 4. However, this introduces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K-1=5$$\end{document} additional hyperparameters requiring calibration data, and overfitting to small calibration sets is a concern. Brier score decomposition into reliability, resolution, and uncertainty components37 should be systematically reported alongside aggregate Brier score to distinguish whether differences reflect calibration quality versus discriminative power.

Bayesian uncertainty quantification approaches could address the limitation that our deterministic neural network provides point estimates without epistemic uncertainty (model uncertainty). Monte Carlo dropout treats dropout as Bayesian approximation: at test time, multiple forward passes with dropout enabled yield a distribution of predictions, and the variance quantifies model uncertainty. Deep ensembles train multiple networks with different random initializations, and ensemble disagreement quantifies model uncertainty. Conformal prediction provides distribution-free frameworks for constructing prediction sets with guaranteed coverage; for example, 90% prediction sets that contain the true stage for 90% of patients. The expected benefit is more reliable uncertainty estimates for borderline cases; patients near stage boundaries such as eGFR = 58 mL/min/1.73 m\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^2$$\end{document} (Stage 2 vs. 3a boundary); enabling ”uncertain” predictions that trigger additional diagnostic testing rather than confident misclassifications.

Strengthening monotonicity guarantees

Our gradient penalty enforces local (differential) monotonicity but does not guarantee global monotonicity under large perturbations or feature interactions. Stronger architectural constraints include lattice networks, which represent piecewise-linear functions with guaranteed monotonicity by construction. Parameters represent function values at lattice vertices, and interpolation between vertices is monotonic if appropriate constraints are satisfied during training. Advantages include provable global monotonicity and interpretable lattice structure, but scalability to high dimensions suffers from curse of dimensionality and the restricted function class may sacrifice discriminative performance.

Min-max architectures construct monotone functions using only monotone operations; max, min, addition, ReLU; guaranteeing monotonicity by construction without gradient penalties. However, the restricted function class limits expressiveness. Monotone splines constrain the network to output spline coefficients with non-negativity constraints, enabling smooth monotone functions with efficient evaluation but limited to univariate or additive multi-variate functions. We recommend a hybrid strategy combining flexible neural networks for most features with monotone sub-networks for critical physiological variables (Scr, urea, age). This balances expressiveness; allowing complex non-linear interactions among hematologic and metabolic features; with guarantees where they matter most for clinical plausibility.

A future hybrid architecture could be constructed in six steps as follows in this paragraph. First, the 13 input features are partitioned into d = 3 monotone-constrained features \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_m$$\end{document} = {Scr, Urea, Age} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_u$$\end{document} = 10 unconstrained features \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_u$$\end{document} comprising the remaining variables. Second, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_m$$\end{document} is passed through a low-dimensional lattice sub-network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M(x_m)$$\end{document}; where each feature is normalised by a piecewise-linear calibrator, the three calibrated values index a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$4^3$$\end{document} = 64-vertex lattice, and a linear projection with non-negative weight matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_m$$\end{document} maps the lattice output to a monotone embedding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_m$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\in$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{R}^{32}$$\end{document}. Global monotonicity is guaranteed by construction via the vertex inequality constraints \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta h_{m,j} / \delta Scr$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ge$$\end{document} 0 for all inputs and all perturbation magnitudes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta$$\end{document} > 0. Third, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_u$$\end{document} is processed by the existing residual MLP trunk i.e. with the stem modified to accept 10 rather than 13 features \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(W_0 \in \textrm{R}^{384 \times 10})$$\end{document}; producing an unconstrained embedding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_u \in \textrm{R}^{384}$$\end{document}. The D = 8 residual blocks, dropout, and all other hyperparameters can remain unchanged (tuned appropiately later). Fourth, the two embeddings are concatenated: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h = [h_m \mid \mid h_u] \in \textrm{R}^{416}$$\end{document}, followed by an optional fusion projection h = ReLU(W_fuse h + b_fuse) back to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{R}^{384}$$\end{document}. Fifth, both task heads receive h as input, exactly as the current student receives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_D$$\end{document}. For the CORN ordinal head, the portion of the weight matrix corresponding to the monotone embedding columns, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\_ord^{(m)} \in \textrm{R}^{5\times 32}$$\end{document}, is constrained to be element-wise non-negative. This ensures that each conditional probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_k = \sigma (W\_ord^{(m)} h_m + W\_ord^{(u)} h_u + b\_ord)$$\end{document} is non-decreasing in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_m$$\end{document}; since cumulative products of non-decreasing functions are non-decreasing, and the expected stage \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{s} = \sum _k k \hat{p}_k$$\end{document} is a non-decreasing function of the cumulative probabilities, global monotonicity propagates end-to-end from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_m$$\end{document} through \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_m$$\end{document} through the CORN head to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{s}$$\end{document}. Sixth, the composite loss and knowledge distillation pipeline are unchanged: the lattice and calibrators are end-to-end differentiable, all four loss components backpropagate normally through both pathways, and the gradient penalty L_mono can optionally be set to zero or retained as a soft complement.

Incorporating albuminuria and complete KDIGO staging

To align with full KDIGO 2012 guidelines2, future work should extend the model to dual-axis staging: predicting both eGFR category (G1–G5) and albuminuria category (A1–A3) simultaneously. A multi-task learning architecture with separate ordinal heads for eGFR-grade and ACR-grade, jointly optimized with cross-task regularization encouraging consistency, could enable comprehensive KDIGO staging. The expected benefit is better risk stratification; for example, distinguishing G2A3 patients (eGFR 60–89 with severely increased albuminuria, high cardiovascular risk) from G3aA1 patients (eGFR 45–59 with normal albuminuria, lower risk despite worse eGFR).

However, albuminuria exhibits high day-to-day variability (coefficient of variation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sim$$\end{document}40%), requiring multiple measurements for reliable classification. Longitudinal ACR modeling using recurrent neural networks (LSTM, GRU) or Transformer architectures could capture temporal trajectories of albuminuria change, improving reliability over single measurements. This requires cohorts with repeated ACR measurements over time, ideally at least three measurements per patient. Joint progression modeling of eGFR and ACR decline trajectories using multi-output survival analysis or longitudinal mixed models integrated with neural networks could better predict progression to ESRD and identify rapid progressors requiring intensive management. These extensions represent natural evolution of the current framework but require richer longitudinal data than available in our cross-sectional cohort.

Decision curve analysis and clinical utility assessment

Beyond discrimination and calibration metrics, clinical utility should be evaluated using decision-analytic frameworks. Decision curve analysis (DCA) quantifies net benefit of model-guided decisions across a range of clinical thresholds. For example, for nephrology referral decisions, DCA plots net benefit versus referral threshold (predicted risk >30%, >40%, >50%), comparing model-guided referral against strategies of ”refer all patients” (maximizes sensitivity but high false-positive rate), ”refer none” (minimizes false positives but misses true cases), and current guideline-based practice (refer if eGFR < 30 or eGFR < 60 with diabetes). We expect the model to add net benefit at intermediate thresholds (30–60% predicted risk) where clinical judgment is most uncertain and where guidelines provide limited guidance.

Clinical impact curves complement DCA by showing the number of high-risk patients identified and number referred per 1000 population at different thresholds, helping clinicians understand workload implications of model-guided care. For example, at 40% threshold, the model might identify 150 per 1000 high-risk patients requiring intensive management while avoiding unnecessary referrals for 200 per 1000 low-risk patients, representing substantial healthcare resource optimization.

Confusion matrices at actionable thresholds should be reported at clinically relevant decision boundaries rather than overall accuracy. For example, the threshold distinguishing Stage 3a versus 3b (eGFR = 45 mL/min/1.73 m\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^2$$\end{document}) is critical for medication dosing adjustment decisions; certain antibiotics, anticoagulants, and chemotherapies require dose reduction at this threshold. Similarly, the threshold distinguishing Stage 4 versus 5 (eGFR = 15 mL/min/1.73 m\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^2$$\end{document}) triggers dialysis access creation and transplant evaluation. Clinical misclassification costs can be quantified as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{i,j} c_{ij} \cdot \text {confusion}[i,j]$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_{ij}$$\end{document} represents the clinical cost of misclassifying stage i as stage j, with costs elicited from nephrologist expert panels. This aligns evaluation metrics directly with clinical consequences rather than treating all errors equally.

EHR integration and real-world deployment

Translating research models to clinical practice requires addressing implementation challenges beyond algorithmic performance. A pragmatic EHR integration pipeline includes four stages. First, pre-deployment external validation on the target population with site-specific recalibration if needed (e.g., temperature scaling fitted on local calibration set). Second, integration into clinical decision support (CDS) workflows, embedding the model into EHR systems to flag high-risk patients during encounters or automatically trigger nephrology referral orders for review. Third, guardrails and fail-safes including confidence thresholds (only display predictions with >80% maximum class probability), monotonicity audits to flag physiologically implausible predictions, and override mechanisms allowing clinicians to dismiss recommendations with documented rationale. Fourth, post-deployment monitoring to track model performance over time, detect dataset shift from changing patient demographics or laboratory calibration drift, and implement scheduled recalibration protocols (e.g., annually or when performance metrics degrade beyond pre-specified thresholds).

Regulatory considerations vary by jurisdiction. In the United States, the FDA classifies medical AI software as medical devices, and our model would likely fall under Class II (moderate risk) as it informs clinical decisions but does not automate treatment. Regulatory approval requires a pivotal validation study in the target population, demonstration of clinical utility (not just statistical performance), comprehensive risk-benefit analysis, and software documentation including intended use, contraindications, and limitations. Post-market surveillance for adverse events and algorithm change control protocols are mandatory. Similar requirements exist in the European Union (CE marking under Medical Device Regulation) and other jurisdictions.

Transparency and explainability are essential for clinical adoption. We recommend following TRIPOD-AI guidelines74 for transparent reporting of clinical prediction models using AI/ML methods. A model card should document intended use population, training data characteristics, performance metrics stratified by subgroups, known limitations, and failure modes. Clinician training programs should educate end-users on when to trust versus override model predictions, how to interpret confidence estimates, and appropriate use cases versus out-of-scope scenarios. Implementation science research; qualitative interviews with clinicians, usability testing, workflow analysis; should inform deployment strategies to maximize adoption and minimize alert fatigue.

Broader implications for medical AI

This study’s core principle; embedding structured clinical knowledge including stage ordering, renal physiology, and calibrated risk directly into model design; offers a template beyond CKD staging. The methodological framework is transferable to other ordinal medical classification tasks where misclassification costs scale with distance between categories.

In cardiovascular disease staging, NYHA functional class (I–IV) classifies heart failure severity, Canadian Cardiovascular Society angina grade (0–IV) classifies chest pain severity, and valvular stenosis grading (none/mild/moderate/severe) guides intervention timing. Predicting severe aortic stenosis when mild is present leads to inappropriate surgery; predicting mild stenosis when severe leads to delayed valve replacement and preventable heart failure. Ordinal neural networks with physiological constraints; for example, enforcing that higher N-terminal pro-BNP should not predict milder NYHA class; could improve reliability.

In cancer staging, the TNM system classifies tumor extent (T), lymph node involvement (N), and metastasis (M) as ordinal categories. Tumor grade (G1–G4 representing well-differentiated to poorly-differentiated histology) and treatment response assessment (complete response, partial response, stable disease, progressive disease) also have natural ordinal structure. Confusing complete response with progressive disease has catastrophic consequences (discontinuing effective therapy), whereas confusing partial response with complete response has modest consequences (continued monitoring instead of immediate surveillance). Ordinal-aware models could concentrate errors between adjacent categories, improving safety.

In neurodegenerative disease, Alzheimer’s Clinical Dementia Rating scale (CDR 0–3) measures cognitive impairment severity, Parkinson’s Hoehn and Yahr scale (1–5) stages motor progression, and ALS Functional Rating Scale tracks physical decline. Progression forecasting; predicting which Mild Cognitive Impairment patients will progress to Alzheimer’s dementia within 3 years; is more clinically relevant than cross-sectional staging and avoids circular reasoning when biomarkers used for staging (e.g., amyloid-PET) are also predictive features.

The combination of ordinal structure to concentrate errors between adjacent categories, probability calibration to enable reliable risk communication, and physiological constraints to ensure clinical plausibility represents a principled approach to developing trustworthy medical AI that aligns with clinical reasoning rather than merely maximizing statistical performance metrics. However, the target leakage issue underscores a critical lesson: high statistical performance does not guarantee clinical utility or scientific validity. Our 0.904 Macro-F1 and 0.991 Macro-AUC represent excellent performance by conventional ML benchmarks, yet the model is fundamentally limited by circular reasoning in its training data. This highlights the necessity of domain expertise in medical AI development; technical sophistication alone cannot overcome flawed problem formulations. Close collaboration between machine learning researchers and clinical domain experts from study design through validation is essential to ensure that models address meaningful clinical questions rather than optimizing statistically convenient but clinically circular tasks.

Conclusion

We present a framework for multi-stage chronic kidney disease (CKD) staging that unites ordinal classification, probability calibration, and physiological monotonicity within a knowledge distillation paradigm. By leveraging a calibrated CatBoost teacher to transfer temperature-scaled probabilities to an ordinal neural network student with an auxiliary eGFR regression head, our approach delivers accurate, well-calibrated, and physiologically plausible stage predictions on a cohort of 750 patients from Al-Ramadi Teaching Hospital. Across multiple performance metrics, the framework consistently outperformed strong baselines while retaining computational efficiency suitable for integration into clinical workflows. The integration of domain knowledge with modern learning techniques offers a template for developing safe, transparent, and effective clinical decision support tools. Broader validation across diverse populations and healthcare systems, and expansion to include additional KDIGO-recommended markers such as albuminuria, will be essential next steps toward real-world adoption. Our findings suggest that principled model design, grounded in both clinical and methodological rigor, can advance the reliability and trustworthiness of artificial intelligence in medicine.
